#2827. It’s time for Basketball: San Antonio Spurs vs Golden State Warriors

Welcome to the Game Thread. Veterans of the Game Thread know how we do things around here, but for all you newbies we have a few rules. Our community guidelines apply and basically say be cool, no personal attacks, don't troll and don't swear too much.

The Spurs are on a double digit win streak and they're back at home tonight after a successful four game road trip. Tonight is an NBA Cup game against the Golden State Warriors for the lead in West Group C. All of the teams in Group C except for the Spurs and Warriors have a loss in the group stage of the Emirates Cup tournament, and one of those teams is going to have a loss after tonight's game. The winner of tonight's game will have the only undefeated record in the group so far, and will win the group outright by winning the last two games. The Spurs' remaining group stage games are at Portland and against the Lakers at home, neither of which counts as an easy win, but a win tonight gives them an inside track on advancing to the knockout rounds and making a little extra money for the guys. Actually, it's a nice payday for the Cup winners, $530,000 for players who are Cup champions, which could be really helpful for the guys on rookie contracts.

The Warriors are missing Steph Curry who tore an oblique on Wednesday night against the Rockets, which means that the best player on the Warriors is going to be super rookie Yaxel Lendeborg, who has been tearing up the league so far this year. Athough Yax is a first year player, he's actually older than three of the starting five players on the Spurs. The Warriors were really smart to draft a player who was ready to contribute immediately to give Draymond and Steph another chance to win a title, and Lendeborg has definitely made an immediate contribution. This is not a game that the Spurs can take for granted, and if they come out with a lackadaisical effort like the first half of the Bulls game, this could be a rough night for the home team. This is a get-back game [first home game after an extended road trip] for the Silver and Black, and those are always dangerous for the home team. Mitch has to have his team ready for tonight, and maybe he should give the halftime speech from Wednesday in the pre-game.

De'Aaron Fox and Devin Vassell are both questionable for tonight's game, so you could see some interesting lineups this evening. This game is on CW 35, DAZN, and Prime, so you have plenty of options to watch. Lets go Spurs, and Wembanyama must dominate Yaxel.

An official website of the United States Government

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced an enforcement action against American Express Company to address, among other things, the firm's failure to sufficiently detect and report certain suspicious activity related to money laundering.

The Board also identified significant deficiencies in how American Express Company's enterprise-wide anti-money laundering program was implemented, in particular at the firm's subsidiary national bank. The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, the primary federal regulator of the firm's national bank, has issued a separate enforcement action against the bank.

The Board's action will help ensure that the firm operates in compliance with all U.S. laws and regulations.

#2823. Devin Booker’s clutch scoring carries Suns amid starting lineup woes

As precious seconds ticked off the clock late in Thursday night's matchup between the Phoenix Suns and Charlotte Hornets, it was abundantly clear that Charlotte was attempting to intentionally foul Devin Booker. And Booker? He was ready and willing to accept that foul.

Unfortunately, head official Scott Foster apparently had his attention elsewhere, despite the action unfolding approximately five feet in front of him. Perhaps he was looking into the front row, spotted Steve Nash, and began reminiscing about the good old days when he and his fellow officials allowed opponents to assault the two-time MVP with little consequence. Maybe he noticed Brandon Rafi sitting courtside and started thinking about calling Rafi Rafi the next time he needs a lawyer. Whatever was occupying his attention, it certainly wasn't the intentional foul taking place directly in front of him.

Booker responded with that familiar piercing stare, the one we've seen countless times when he's wondering how an official could possibly miss something so obvious. But there was still a basketball game to finish, and with Sion James approaching to provide a double team, Booker decided to take matters into his own hands. He dribbled left, rose up, and buried a 28-footer that effectively sealed the victory for Phoenix.

No whistle? No problem. Sometimes the best response to questionable officiating is to make the officials irrelevant. And in that moment, Devin Booker did exactly that.

That moment encapsulated just how great Devin Booker has been to begin this season, with the exception of his three-point performance against Oklahoma City. For a team navigating a discombobulated, duplicative roster that is ultimately going to go as far as Booker can carry it, the Suns' franchise cornerstone has been delivering. Nowhere has that been more evident than in the clutch.

Booker scored nine points in clutch time against Charlotte, continuing an impressive stretch of late-game execution. Through four clutch appearances this season, he's shooting 66.7% from the field, 50% from beyond the arc, and has a +8 plus/minus. Those are encouraging numbers for a player whose late-game efficiency became a legitimate concern last season.

What impressed me most against the Hornets wasn't necessarily the scoring. It was his decision-making. Every time Charlotte attempted to throw a double team his way, Booker recognized what was happening and responded decisively and appropriately. On one possession, he drove away from the pressure, penetrated the defense, and kicked the ball to Jordan Goodwin in the left corner for an open three-point opportunity. Goodwin missed the shot, but the process was exactly what you wanted to see. On another possession, Booker beat the double team himself and converted. He wasn't forcing the issue. He was reading the defense, understanding what was available, and making the appropriate decision.

We are currently witnessing what can only be described as a Devin Booker heater. Over his last 7 games, he's averaging 27.4 points on 49/40/95 splits.

Quite honestly, the Suns desperately need it because the rest of the starting lineup is struggling mightily. Perhaps it's the lack of continuity and limited playing time together. Maybe it's the absence of clearly defined roles, with players still attempting to understand where they fit within an offense that features overlapping skill sets. Whatever the explanation, the production from Phoenix's starters has been inconsistent and inefficient.

Booker has accounted for 35.1% of all points scored by the Suns' starting lineup this season. Think about that. More than one out of every three points produced by the starters has come from a single player. Meanwhile, the starters not named Devin Booker have combined to shoot 52-of-154 from beyond the arc, a pedestrian 33.8%. In the clutch, everyone without 'Booker' written on their back has combined to make 20% of their threes and 30% of their shots.

While the shooting inefficiencies that plagued Booker last season generated plenty of concern, those issues haven't carried over into the early portion of this campaign. Against Charlotte, he connected on 4-of-8 attempts from deep, continuing to provide the offensive production and efficiency that Phoenix needs from its best player. He's up to 25.5 points this season, doing so on 50/38/94 splits, adding 5.0 assists, and 1.2 steals.

Until the rest of the starting five figures out who they are, what their responsibilities should be, and how they can contribute more effectively on both ends of the floor, the Suns are going to need Booker to keep this heater going. Because if he has an ordinary night, there's a pretty good chance Phoenix is going to lose. That's an unfortunate reality, but it's where this organization finds itself.

Devin Booker is in the prime of his career. This should be the period when the franchise has a clear understanding of what works alongside him, what complements his strengths, and how to construct a roster that maximizes his abilities. The organization has had plenty of time to figure that out.

Instead, we've watched Phoenix spend much of his prime fumbling through roster construction, repeatedly acquiring players with overlapping skill sets and similar positional responsibilities. Rather than consistently surrounding Booker with complementary pieces, the organization has continued placing additional weight on his shoulders, asking him to compensate for deficiencies that should have been addressed through better roster construction. Thankfully, at least through the opening weeks of this season, Booker appears more than willing to accept that responsibility.

But how long can he sustain it? How long can a team reasonably expect one player to account for more than a third of its starting lineup's scoring while simultaneously serving as its primary offensive organizer and late-game closer? At some point, the supporting cast has to establish its identity and begin providing consistent production.

I've been quick to remind people that Devin Booker is the $57 million man whenever his performances fall short of expectations. When you're earning that kind of money, there's a certain level of production, leadership, and accountability that comes with the paycheck. And right now? He's earning every damn dollar.

You earn that money by delivering in the clutch, which Booker has done. You earn it by becoming the stabilizing force that prevents an inconsistent team from completely unraveling. Check that too, as the team sits at 5-6. You earn it by showing up night after night, accepting the responsibility of carrying an offense while your teammates are still trying to figure out their roles, their responsibilities, and perhaps why they're being asked to occupy spaces already filled by somebody else. That's what Devin Booker has been doing.

And while I certainly hope the rest of the roster begins providing him with the support he deserves, there's something worth appreciating about watching a player embrace that burden and continue delivering. Because for all the questions surrounding the Phoenix Suns right now, Devin Booker is providing the most important answer. He's playing like the $57 million man.

We've spent plenty of time questioning Booker's late-game execution, his decision-making, and whether he can consistently elevate a flawed roster. Right now, he's answering those questions with his play. The Suns still have plenty to figure out, and Booker can't be expected to carry them indefinitely. But for the moment, he's reminding everyone why he's the face of this franchise. And why, when the game is on the line, you probably shouldn't leave him open from 28 feet.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2822. After Jensen Huang’s exit, what top financial advisors are doing with Nvidia: Exclusive CNBC poll

Shares of Nvidia continued to slide after the market open on Friday after declining 4% on Thursday following news of co-founder Jensen Huang's resignation as chief executive, amid reports about his personal relationships and national security concerns. The news has raised questions for investors about what comes next for a company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

To gauge how wealth managers are responding, CNBC polled members of its Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council. Thirty of the 122 firms and professionals contacted responded to the online, multiple-choice flash poll, conducted between 11 a.m. and 5 p.m. EST on Nov. 12. The poll is a snapshot of respondents' views, not a measure of all financial advisors.

Most respondents, 19, said they are holding their Nvidia positions steady. Another four said they are increasing their stake, while seven said they do not own the stock. None said they were selling at the time of the poll.

"Most of our clients are in a position to hold; the reason is because it makes up a smaller percentage of an overall diversified portfolio," Winnie Sun, managing director and founding partner of Irvine, California-based Sun Group Wealth Partners, said in an interview. Sun is a member of the CNBC Financial Advisor Council.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

In open responses, several advisors wrote that they are taking a "wait-and-see" approach before changing their investment strategy with Nvidia and are telling their clients to do the same.

"Don't react to short-term headlines," wrote certified financial planner Peyton Jamison, president of Jamison Private Wealth Management in Alpharetta, Georgia. "The company's valuation is extremely attractive, and the focus should remain on its underlying fundamentals and long-term investment potential." His firm is ranked No. 78 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Asked what factors could change their approach to Nvidia, 13 respondents cited the outlook for AI demand, 11 pointed to valuation, and 10 mentioned company leadership or governance. Some said the decision depends on the client, with eight citing risk tolerance and seven mentioning portfolio concentration. Seven said they do not expect to change their approach.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results Tuesday, after the market close. In poll remarks, advisors said the company's fiscal third-quarter earnings will help determine their course of action.

Advisors said that for investors, what to do may depend on how much Nvidia stock they already own, when they expect to need the money and how comfortable they are with volatility.

"If this is a company that's going to keep you up at night and you just feel really uncomfortable with these unknown risks, then there was always the option to sell and move that capital to another stock or another portfolio," Sun said.

Nvidia exposure can also come through mutual funds and ETFs. Nvidia's large market value makes it a significant holding in many broad-market funds, including funds that track the S&P 500, as well as target-date funds in retirement-plan options.

Investors can check their Nvidia exposure by reviewing the fact sheets for the funds in their 401(k) or brokerage accounts, which typically list a fund's largest holdings. A stock's decline does not translate one-for-one into a fund's decline.

However, investors with dedicated technology, semiconductor or growth funds may have more Nvidia exposure than they realize, advisors say.

"Nvidia is a big part of virtually everyone's portfolio whether they take an active or passive index approach," wrote Andy Pratt, a partner and director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia, which is ranked No. 34 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

He said he advises clients not to overreact to short-term news and to let the dust settle before making decisions.

— CNBC's Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

#2821. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2820. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#2818. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2817. Rising rents draw Wall Street money to industrial outdoor storage lot investments across the U.S.

Nondescript lots holding idle bulldozers, trucks and other industrial equipment are a common sight in local real estate markets across the U.S., and increasingly, a focus for Wall Street private investment giants.

If you haven't noticed them, it's probably because they so easily fade into the landscape, just another non-branded bit of land in the miasma of suburbia. But Brookfield and Blackstone, two of the world's largest alternative asset managers, are among the institutional investors moving in on what the industry calls industrial outdoor storage, or IOS. Investors are eyeing these lots as the AI build-out increases the demand for heavy equipment.

"We have strong conviction in industrial outdoor storage, which plays a critical role in our supply chain," said Devin Barnwell, a managing partner and co-leader of Brookfield's logistics business.

He says that up until now, the segment has been fragmented, but Brookfield sees that changing.

"We see significant opportunity to bring institutional capital and operating expertise to this fragmented sector, improving the quality and efficiency of these assets while strengthening the resilience of our industrial supply chains," Barnwell said.

Brookfield spent $1.2 billion earlier this year to take Peakstone Realty Trust private, a deal completed in May that gave it a portfolio of more than 70 industrial properties, most of them outdoor storage yards.

Blackstone is also ramping up in the sector, mainly as a lender. Its real estate debt arm has made six loans to outdoor storage owners totaling more than $1.1 billion, including a $244 million loan in June to Alterra IOS to buy 37 properties.

Despite the billions being bet on the market by Wall Street, there is still opportunity in the market for individual investors and local entrepreneurs looking to park their money where others park their equipment.

"1,000 percent yes," said Adam Petrillo, an executive managing director at Newmark, a commercial real estate advisory firm, who co-leads its industrial outdoor storage practice.

Petrillo said smaller, non-institutional investors, still control a significant amount of well-placed storage in secondary markets. "The good news for smaller investors and mom and pops is that a good individual IOS site isn't necessarily 50 acres," Petrillo said.

Lots as small as three to five acres in primary or secondary markets can still yield a robust return, he said.  "At that bite size, an individual can step in. We are not talking about tens of millions ... a three-acre, well-located IOS with multiple use cases can be found, marketed, leased,  and stabilized," he added.

Rents for industrial outdoor storage roughly doubled across eight major markets between 2020 and the third quarter of this year, according to data from Newmark Research and Alterra IOS, an owner of such properties. Average rent per usable acre rose 103.5%, to nearly $11,000 a month, compared with a 66% increase for traditional industrial space, such as indoor warehouses.

Rent growth, according to Alterra, is most closely tied to infrastructure and energy activity in the area, even more so than data center development specifically.

The value of these properties can also increase if located near a seaport, rail line, or highway. But the gains weren't uniform. Baltimore (204%), Houston (159%) and Norfolk (148%) led, while South Florida and Savannah IOS actually trailed traditional industrial.

Lisa DeNight, managing director, head of North American industrial research at Newmark, said that Savannah's IOS vacancy remains quite tight despite the rent figures. Recent port activity is a potential factor, according to Alterra. Savannah's container volumes more than doubled between 2010 and 2022 but have since declined, while Houston saw roughly 15% growth from 2022 to 2025.

Petrillo said that there is still plenty of room left for values to increase, so an investor jumping in now hasn't missed the game. "We are still in the very early innings," Petrillo said.

Sam Cheema co-founder of CanTex Capital, a Dallas-based firm that acquires overlooked industrial real estate in Texas, is seeing increased demand for these lots.

"It comes down to supply and demand," he said. "If you need a warehouse you can usually find one. Properly zoned infill IOS sites are much harder to find and every year more of them are converted to warehouses and other uses," he said, referencing multifamily housing units. The scarcity makes tenants sticky. "So a limited supply keeps shrinking. And because so much of the value is in the land itself, owners keep the option to redevelop the site as the area grows," Cheema added.

Because of the imbalance of supply and demand, rents and prices are rising across the board, according to Cheema, but no two IOS properties look alike, which has kept the door open for smaller investors.

"Big institutions want exposure to IOS, but ownership is fragmented across mom-and-pop owners and small businesses, so it's hard to deploy large amounts of capital without first aggregating these assets into portfolios," Cheema said.

Unlike traditional warehouses, there's no standard spec like square footage or clear height to compare them. "That lack of standardization along with the ongoing growth of the asset class, means there's still runway for small investors to find opportunities," Cheema said.

"Financing has improved as more lenders have a better understanding of IOS, but it's still largely local and regional banks," Cheema added.

"If you don't understand the city's plans for the area, you may end up owning a use they're trying to phase out," Cheema said.

CanTex Capital works with the city and neighborhoods before buying to ensure the impact aligns with the community's plan for its future.

Alex Sutterer, an executive vice president at NAI Hiffman, a Chicago-area commercial real estate firm, said that beyond being a place to store big equipment, IOS attracts investors for two main reasons: lower capital expenditure requirements than traditional industrial or multifamily properties and the potential for higher yields.

"Capital improvements are also less specific to individual tenants, so owners can direct spending toward the property's overall functionality," Sutterer said.

For private investors, IOS typically trades at a yield around one percentage point higher than traditional industrial properties, helping attract smaller investors and family offices. "We believe that gap will continue to narrow as more institutional capital enters this space," Sutterer added.

Even for industrial properties with similar yields, he said the credit profile of tenants is generally stronger for IOS.

Shallow-bay, multitenant industrial properties — industrial facilities that tend to be much smaller than big-box distribution warehouses — have comparable yields. But unlike the local businesses that tend to fill up shallow-bay sites, IOS tenants range from local businesses to those with investment-grade credit ratings, offering investors the potential for both yield and creditworthy tenants.

And institutional money is making IOS more competitive.

"Institutional capital has increased competition in IOS, and property values and lease rates have risen overall," Sutterer said.

He added smaller investors can still find opportunities below institutional deal-size thresholds, although that does not mean every smaller deal is priced fairly. "We see opportunities to add value and earn yield in deals below $3 million–$5 million," Sutterer said.

For local investors, that means there is the ability to build a portfolio at a smaller scale and hold the assets for cash flow, or aggregate more than $10 million in assets and seek a portfolio premium when selling to institutional capital.

But buyers need to understand what they are getting into. Due diligence on tenant demand by site and site function, and tenant turnover ratio, are important factors in buying the right asset. Market diversity can also provide protection, Sutterer said.

"In Chicago, no single user category accounts for more than 18% of IOS tenants, helping insulate the market from industry-specific slowdowns," he said. Tenants' tendency to stay and tight vacancy rates nationally are current advantages of the asset class. In Chicagoland, for example, vacancy is below 3%.

Still, all the due diligence in the world can't protect investors from some variables.

"No one outside the Federal Reserve can control the cost of debt," said Kellie McIvor, vice president of acquisitions at Redwood Living, an Ohio real estate development firm. She said that right now, the cost of debt for light industrial uses is lower than, say, for residential construction.

"However, interest rates are still high, with no relief in sight," McIvor said.

The Fed is expected to raise rates in December in its effort to tame inflation, and after this week's latest hot October CPI report, potentially hike twice more in early 2027, according to some Wall Street forecasts.

"Because lenders view them as riskier than a large institutional investor, smaller buyers can face more expensive debt and may be asked to bring more equity to the deal. That can make financing a small acquisition disproportionately expensive. ... More expensive debt means less room for error in execution," McIvor said.

A small buyer looking at a small site that requires no improvement may be able to fund the deal all on his own. "That's the dream! Own your business and the land it's on, outright!" she added.

Ross Blankenship, who owns two fenced contractor and fleet yards in Colorado's Vail Valley through Wins Parking, said the math has to work today. In a mountain market where little land is zoned for the use, he rents spaces for $210 a month for a car or pickup and $265 for a box truck or trailer, and charges $1,250 to $5,500 a month for contractor zones. Blankenship also runs Park Graph, the parking software he uses to rent the spaces.

The swing factor is vacancy, he said. "A full yard throws off more cash per dollar of capex than a small retail or office building. An empty one earns nothing," he said.

His advice for a first-time buyer: call 10 local contractors and ask what they pay for space.

"A small investor can still buy, but the deal has to work on rents you can collect today, not on what an institution might pay you later," Blankenship said.

#2816. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2814. They train people to avoid 'honeypot' scams—here are the common red flags they spot: 'It feels so good to be wanted'

Shawnee Delaney often kicks off a training session by telling a room full of corporate workers: "OK, raise your hand if you've ever gotten a flattering message on LinkedIn just complimenting your background and your profile and your experience."

"Every hand goes up," she says. Then, she tells attendees how responding to a simple adulatory message could unwittingly open the door to a potential romance scam.

Delaney is a corporate security expert, well-versed in teaching people how to avoid “honeypot” schemes, in which a bad actor uses romantic or sexual interest to build trust and gain information, access or leverage over a target. The term is currently making headlines in the wake of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation from the company, following reporting on his admitted affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Honeypots, also known as "honey traps," are a very human form of social engineering attack, a category that also includes various forms of phishing and use of deepfake technology. And you don't have to be a billionaire tech CEO to possess information that scammers may value.

As competition in the global artificial intelligence race heats up, OpenAI is reportedly training employees to recognize espionage tactics including bribery, romantic manipulation and blackmail, according to The Wall Street Journal. On Nov. 3, cryptocurrency exchange Binance revealed that a recent cyberattack — which saw roughly $6 billion of digital assets stolen — was partially made possible by a fake job interview scheme that infected an employee’s work computer with malware.

"People can get solicited, bribed, romanced, etc.," says Kevin Tian, co-founder and CEO of Doppel, an AI-powered platform meant to help identify and deflect social engineering attacks. He adds: "If you've got access to sensitive IP [intellectual property] — it's not just AI companies — you can be a target."

Increasingly, scammers can use AI to scour websites ranging from Reddit to Pinterest for an individual's personal information and customize a ploy designed specifically for them, Tian says. Once they have relevant data points, attackers tend to use "the oldest tricks in the book: elicitation, flattery, ego," says Delaney, a former intelligence officer with the Defense Intelligence Agency and the founder and CEO of Washington, D.C.-based corporate security consulting firm Vaillance Group.

In many cases, Delaney notes, targets have an inkling that something is suspicious — but they'd rather allow themselves to believe the interest is legitimate. So how do you keep yourself from falling victim? Trust your self-awareness, common sense and ability to identify situations that feel too good to be true, she says.

"The bottom line is, we're all human," says Delaney, adding: "It feels so good to be wanted, or to be respected, or to be appreciated, or loved."

Delaney, who has worked with Fortune 500 companies such as Uber and Merck, advises corporate clients on cybersecurity risks and develops training programs to improve employees' awareness of security threats. Her sessions aim to build awareness of manipulative connections, such as romance scams, from how they begin to the natural progression they often take. "Introduction, then repeated contact, favors, sensitive requests — the secrecy, the pressure," she says.

Bad actors may tap into any information you post online to find an interest that can be mirrored and exploited, or an acquaintance who can be infiltrated and make an introduction. If someone you know and trust makes an introduction, you might be less likely to "go do due diligence" on the new person, even if they're a stranger, Delaney says.

One of Vaillance's services includes building a "targeting package" around an executive or C-Suite member, says Delaney. "We can show how they are vulnerable and how we would target that organization just by leveraging the people around them in that circle: spouses, friends, kids, parents, administrative assistants, neighbors," she says. "That's when people's eyes open."

From there, she discusses red flags to spot. Conversations that begin with flattery and seemingly innocuous small talk can gradually shift toward digging for information, introductions to colleagues or access to documents, says Delaney. It's human nature to want to be helpful and fulfill friendly requests, she adds. Add a potential romantic connection to the mix, especially amid an ongoing "loneliness epidemic," and the urge to comply can be even harder to resist, she says.

If you feel yourself getting cornered in a situation where a recent acquaintance or romantic partner keeps asking for information you're not comfortable revealing, Delaney says it helps to have first practiced polite refusal. "One of my favorites is: They could just respond with: 'Why do you ask?'" she says. "Put the other person on the defensive. You could give a nondescript answer. You can say you don't know. You can say you can't respond."

If you're ever suspicious of a new connection — even if you don't want to be — do yourself a favor and conduct some basic vetting, she recommends. You might examine their social media profiles and other publicly available information, for example, to see if their story checks out.

If you can't find enough information to allay your suspicions, don't be shy about alerting your employer's security or human resources team, she says. For smaller companies, or scams outside the workplace, you can file a complaint for cyber threats or romance scams with the Federal Bureau of Investigation's Internet Crime Complaint Center.

And pay attention if your new paramour starts asking you specific questions about your finances or work credentials, says Delaney.

"If you have no game at home, and you get off an airplane somewhere and suddenly everyone's all over you, we call that a red flag," she says.

#2812. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2811. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2809. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2806. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2803. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2802. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.
