#2761. ‘Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles

'Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Anfernee Simons and Jabari Walker shared the floor for 128 games in Portland. The Trail Blazers won 43 of them.

They sat beside one another in the Sixers' interview room Thursday night after a victory and discussed what the other 85 taught them.

"Whether you've got a big role or a small role, it's hard to win in the NBA," Simons said. "We've seen it firsthand."

Their roles were considerably different then.

Simons was an established starter entrusted with creating and scoring. Walker, a 2022 second-round pick, was trying to establish himself in a rotation. During their three overlapping seasons, Portland went 33-49, 21-61 and 36-46. The Blazers missed the playoffs each year.

Both are now reserves on a Sixers team constructed to contend. In nine games they've both appeared in this season, Philadelphia is 7-2.

They provided 26 points, 11 rebounds and six three-pointers in the Sixers' 123-112 win over the Raptors at Xfinity Mobile Arena. Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range. Walker recorded nine points and eight rebounds, including four on the offensive glass.

The Sixers outscored Toronto by 24 points during Simons' 23 minutes. Neither former Blazer committed a turnover.

Their production helped Philadelphia overcome a 17-point first-quarter deficit and improve to 10-3 without LeBron James.

"You learn once you come off the bench to kind of read the game before you get in there," Simons said. "Obviously, we started off kind of slow. You just want to go in, bring some energy, get some stops, get out and run. I think everybody who got off the bench brought energy."

Simons supplied an immediate lift with a deep three-pointer at the first-quarter buzzer. His shot ended an 8-0 Sixers run and trimmed Toronto's lead to nine.

He remained involved during an 11-0 burst late in the third quarter, knocking down a three and setting up Walker for a basket. Walker then made a three early in the fourth before Simons scored on the Sixers' next possession.

When Toronto pulled within six with less than five minutes remaining, Simons answered with his fifth three-pointer.

Making those shots is the most obvious way Simons can help a roster already featuring James, Joel Embiid, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown. He said the Sixers have not made him feel as though every opportunity must go in.

"They encourage us to keep shooting the ball," Simons said. "Knowing that a team has that much confidence in us to keep shooting makes the game that much easier. Whether you miss or make, as long as we get the right shots in the right moments, I think everybody can live with the misses and makes."

Simons spent seven seasons in Portland before playing last year with Boston and Chicago. His move to Philadelphia reunited him with Walker and Rayan Rupert, another former Blazer who joined the Sixers on a two-way contract during the offseason.

Knowing familiar faces awaited him helped Simons enter another new locker room.

"It made the transition seamless," he said. "Just being able to have some comfortability with guys that I played with for multiple years. It's been fun, kind of feeling like a little bit of old times. Me, Bari and Rups have been through a lot."

Walker's path to Philadelphia began a year earlier and required an additional step.

After playing 188 games over three seasons with Portland, Walker signed a two-way contract with the Sixers in July 2025. He earned a standard contract in February and has since pushed his way into the backup-center conversation despite standing 6-foot-7.

Walker believes some of the work he did on losing teams was easily missed.

"When I first got to the league, I was part of a rebuilding process," he said. "I just felt like some of the little things I did didn't always get noticed when you're losing. I told myself, if I ever got to be part of a winning organization, then I'd go out and expand my abilities."

Rebounding has made Walker difficult for Nick Nurse to overlook.

Walker credited his timing and strong hands to lessons passed down by his father, former NBA center Samaki Walker. Those instincts have become especially useful while the Sixers piece together their frontcourt behind Embiid.

"Rebounding is a skill I kind of honed in on," Walker said. "My dad was a good rebounder. He was known for his rebounding and his timing. I think it's just a gift that he transferred to me."

Walker has made it clear that he expected to earn a role this season.

"I don't know if I was delusional, but I always felt like I was going to play and I was going to be a part," he said. "I read something where they said it's a surprise, but if you were around me this summer, it wouldn't be a surprise. This is expected, and I just want to continue to get better."

The freedom Simons once had in Portland is no longer necessary. Walker does not need a high volume of touches, either. Both understand that playing beside the Sixers' stars requires shooting when open, moving the ball and handling the less glamorous work.

"You've got to find ways to impact the game multiple ways, even if you're not scoring," Simons said. "Whether it's cutting, making the right read and pass to Joel, being the right swing pass to VJ, boxing out on defense or just being in the right position."

Their opportunities will fluctuate. Simons may take nine shots one night and only a few the next. Walker's minutes will depend on matchups and the health of Philadelphia's frontcourt.

Winning has changed what they are being asked to do. Their three years together in Portland taught them why the adjustment is worthwhile.

"You feel appreciative of the times where you go into a situation where your role might be a little bit less, but you're winning," Simons said. "That's something you can't take for granted."

#2759. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2757. Nvidia board urged to consider 'potential return of Jensen Huang' by two major investors

Two major Nvidia shareholders are calling on the board of directors to weigh all options for the company's future leadership, including "the potential return of Jensen Huang."

Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner posted a letter on X on Friday that urges Nvidia's board to "pursue a slow, measured, and deliberate process regarding the future leadership of Nvidia." The letter is also signed by Atreides Management's Gavin Baker.

Nvidia was Altimeter Capital's largest disclosed equity holding in its most recent SEC filing, with a 9.4 million-share stake worth nearly $1.9 billion as of June 30. Atreides held 1.2 million shares worth nearly $250 million at the end of June.

The letter said Nvidia will be "capably led" by interim CEO Colette Kress, who served as Nvidia's CFO for 13 years before the board announced Thursday that she'd lead the company in Huang's absence.

"But the Founder and long time incredible leader of this extraordinary company has earned the right for a deliberate process that avoids any needless rush to replace him before all the facts are known," the letter concludes. The document did not call for Huang's immediate reinstatement.

Nvidia founder Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday amid multiple affair allegations, including one with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who also worked with Nvidia customer Megaspeed. The Singapore-based company is being investigated by the Justice Department for possibly redirecting some of its supply of Nvidia's restricted artificial intelligence chips to China.

SpaceX CEO Elon Musk said on X Friday that he signed a petition asking to reinstate Huang. A petition circulating online called "Stand with Jensen" claims to have more than 100,000 signatures, including more than 5,000 current Nvidia employees.

Musk said on X Saturday that without Huang, some "critical deals will be lost."

Another letter sent Friday called on U.S. Secretary of Commerce Howard Lutnick to testify about Megaspeed before the House Intelligence Committee.

Lutnick has agreed to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 10 about whether the Commerce Department is "properly performing its broader role of preventing China from acquiring advanced AI chips," according to the letter. Huang has also been asked to testify.

The Commerce Department originally launched an investigation into Megaspeed that was moved to the Justice Department in 2025 after Nvidia's lawyer contacted a senior Commerce official, according to The New York Times.

President Donald Trump is scheduled to visit China on Thursday, but doesn't plan to raise Nvidia and the suspected spy affair during his meeting with Chinese president Xi Jinping, according to U.S. officials. Musk is expected to join Trump on the trip to Shenzhen, China, for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit on Nov. 18 and 19.

#2755. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2753. Nationwide ground stop followed contractor upgrade to L3Harris telecommunication system: DOT’s Duffy

The longest halt in flight departures to U.S. airports since 9/11 came after updates to a telecommunications system made by L3Harris Technologies, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said Saturday, as travel disruptions lingered for thousands of passengers nationwide.

There was "zero evidence" that the problem was caused by something "nefarious" or anything related to artificial intelligence, Bryan Bedford, the head of the Federal Aviation Administration, said at a press conference.

The updates were part of ongoing work of switching from a copper wire system to fiberoptic cable, Duffy said. When a router was switched to fiberoptic cable from copper, it had "flooded" other routers in the air traffic control system with over 1,000 connection attempts roughly every five seconds, Duffy said, creating problems with airplane communications and radar feeds.

Bedford said no FAA facilities went dark but that officials decided to ground flights because of "instability" in information feeds to air traffic control centers that handle en route aircraft at high altitudes and eventually to others closer to airports.

"We were unwilling to take any risk that this propagation could actually result in a center losing connectivity with aircraft," said Bedford.

L3Harris didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

Duffy told the press conference that the FAA is investigating.

"What we're working to determine was whether we had incompetence, meaning was it user error, installator error from L3 Harris, or did they install a faulty router," he said.

Flight disruptions continued on Saturday. More than 3,700 U.S. flights were canceled Saturday and close to 2,000 were delayed as of 9:40 a.m. ET, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware. That's on top of about 9,000 U.S. flight cancellations and more than 7,000 delays on Friday, though some disruptions were due to bad weather.

Part of untangling from the ground stop includes getting aircraft where they need to be. The ground stop forced many planes to divert to other airports.

Airlines run one of the lightest schedules on Saturday and are deploying as many spare planes and crews as possible to get travelers and employees where they need to be as they're eager to start fresh for the upcoming Thanksgiving holiday period.

American Airlines, for example, is offering flight attendants extra pay to pick up trips, according to a person familiar with the airline's plans.

Delta Air Lines was offering crews double pay to pick up trips and gave them confirmed seats on flights to get them into position, according to internal company messages seen by CNBC. United Airlines was also offering pilots and flight attendants extra pay to pick up flights through Sunday, according to people familiar with the matter.

American and other airlines said they would change customers' tickets at no fee or fare difference if the outage affected their plans.

Around 3 million people were scheduled to fly on Saturday, according to aviation data firm Cirium.

The FAA had halted departing U.S. flights around 4 p.m. ET on Friday, a ground stop that lasted more than four hours, the longest such pause on departing flights in 25 years.

Duffy on Friday blamed outdated U.S. infrastructure for the problem and said Congress needs to approve more money to overhaul the country's air traffic control. Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have angered airline executives for years, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

It was the second time the FAA has issued a nationwide ground stop since the Sept. 11, 2001, terror attacks. Trump administration officials have repeatedly said it did not appear to be a cyberattack.

The U.S. has the world's busiest airspace.

"Before clearing flights to resume, we thoroughly tested our systems to confirm they were stable and that operations remain reliable," the FAA said late Friday.

The disruption also upended travel for NBA, NHL and college teams, which struggled to reach their destinations in time for games this weekend.

Duffy said college football teams traveling on charter flights could receive priority.

"I would encourage those football teams to reach out to the FAA to schedule their flight times and make sure they have games going off tomorrow," he said during a Friday press briefing.

The Southeastern Conference announced on X that some games had been moved to Sunday due to the flight disruption.

"We are in close communication with our member institutions to assess potential disruptions and coordinate appropriate responses," the SEC said in a statement shared on X.

The National Football League said that it is putting in three backup referees for three games on Sunday because of the ground stop, "reassigning officials to games within driving distance of their hometowns."

— CNBC's April Roach and Jessica Golden contributed to this article.

#2752. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

#2751. Sixers, Brown have a ‘humbling' night in loss to Celtics

Sixers, Brown have a 'humbling' night in loss to Celtics originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Whatever Jaylen Brown and the Sixers dreamed up for their first matchup against the Celtics soon flew out the window on Tuesday night.

Brown was whistled for two early fouls, the Sixers fell behind and their early-season flaws were on full display in a 123-101 loss at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

"We've just got to get better," Brown said. "Tonight was humbling. We got outplayed, out-executed. So we go back to the drawing board and get ready for the next one."

Over 26 minutes, Brown had 13 points on 4-for-9 shooting and five rebounds against the team that traded him away this summer.

As for the endless dissection of his relationship with former co-star Jayson Tatum, Brown didn't have much to add Tuesday. There was no public reconciliation, no obvious on-court animosity and generally nothing to satisfy fans hoping for some kind of fireworks on that front.

"I'm just focused on doing what's best for my team," Brown said. "Everything else … to be honest, I wasn't paying attention. But credit to Jayson; he played well tonight. Credit to the Celtics. They executed, they shot the hell out of the ball and they were the better team tonight."

Tatum scored 21 points, two of which came on a third-quarter slam over LeBron James. Paul George continued his fantastic start as a Celtic by dropping 36 points and draining six three-pointers.

The Celtics dominated from beyond the arc, shooting 22 for 49 (44.9 percent). The Sixers went just 11 for 37 (29.7 percent) on another night of underwhelming offense. The team sits 22nd in the NBA in offensive rating, 24th in three-point makes per game and 21st in three-point percentage.

"I think that we've got some guys that are obviously shooting much lower percentages than their last season or their career average — almost a handful of guys," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "And I don't think it's because they're shooting fallaway, totally contested ones. I think they're shots we want them to take. So I think they'll continue to take 'em and I'm pretty sure those will even out into their career averages."

Joel Embiid is at 37 percent from the floor and 4 for 33 from three-point range (12.1 percent).

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center got through an injury scare when he rolled his ankle late in the second quarter and hit a three early in the third. It was his first long-range make since the Sixers' home opener back on Oct. 22.

"It's a work in progress," Embiid said. "I think the biggest thing is just not thinking. I'm probably overthinking it. The rhythm of the game is different. I'm so used to having to create for myself, so it's a little bit different.

"But I think I'm slowly getting better. You could see as soon as I made a three, the next shot in the short corner … I didn't even think about it. I just shot it and let it fly, and I made it. … I think it's just about taking what's in front of you. Make or miss, keep working on your game. It's going to get there at some point."

Even with the Sixers second in the Eastern Conference standings at 9-3, Embiid believes the team's defense has "slacked off a little bit" in recent games.

"I don't really look at the record," he said. "I just look at the process of, 'Are we getting better every day?' We can always do a better job at all phases of the game."

"They're one of the top teams in the league and they've been together for a while," Brown said. "We've got a lot to work on over here, so we've got to get to work."

The Sixers will face the Raptors on Thursday night in Philadelphia.

They'll have to wait until Jan. 21 in Boston to see the Celtics again.

"I was looking forward to this," Brown said. "I was looking forward to playing well, but s— happens. Just get ready for the next one."

#2750. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2747. Back in Philadelphia with Celtics, Paul George looks forward to ‘a regular game'

Back in Philadelphia with Celtics, Paul George looks forward to 'a regular game' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Paul George's presence back in town was one of the many swirling morning storylines at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

For good reason, former Celtics teammates Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum will draw immense attention Tuesday night. After all, Brown has said there "might be a little lamb chops with some gravy" between the two, if not a full-blown beef.

But George's return to Philadelphia is more than a tiny subplot. Following two seasons in Philadelphia, he was the centerpiece of the Sixers' summer trade package to acquire Brown.

George told reporters before the Celtics' shootaround that it feels like "a regular game."

"Had I been here eight, nine, 10 years, different story," he said. "I was here two years. Obviously, we didn't have the best of luck or had a great, I think, partnership in terms of my ability to stay healthy and on the floor. So that kind of played into a lot of it.

"But obviously, it's a tremendous fan base, a great sports town to play in. So that was appreciated. There's no ill will for me. It'll be fun to just compete. And outside of that, it's a normal game."

The 36-year-old George indeed had trouble staying available for sustained stretches as a Sixer. He dealt with a variety of injuries and served a 25-game suspension last season for violating the NBA's anti-drug policy.

George did play well on both ends of the floor for much of the 2026 playoffs. He helped the Sixers come back to win a seven-game first-round series against the Celtics in historic fashion. The nine-time All-Star also brought a veteran steadiness to the Sixers' locker room and was glad to mentor younger players like VJ Edgecombe.

"Just his routine … even when he got hot, he didn't try to force it," Jabari Walker said Tuesday morning. "His game was smooth and I think that's how he is as a person, just not forcing anything and having a calm energy."

George has certainly enjoyed his early days as a Celtic.

Through nine games, he's averaged 20.4 points, 5.2 rebounds, 4.1 assists and 2.3 steals while shooting 42.5 percent from three-point range. Boston will enter Tuesday night's matchup second in the Eastern Conference standings at 8-2, the Sixers first at 9-2.

"I've been kind of blown away," George said. "I think it exceeded my expectations since being in Boston. You never know how much culture is involved and how much culture changes your outlook on things and the effect it has mentally, physically, just being in an environment … and the culture that Boston presents.

"So I've been thrilled. I've been playing some of my best basketball of late, and just been enjoying grinding it out with these guys out here. It's a fun style of play and I can't talk (enough) about just the thrill, the happiness and the excitement and joy that it has brought being here."

He was asked what sort of reception he expects from Sixers fans.

"Not sure," George said. "In some ways, I helped land them a future piece for them. So with that, not sure how the reception would be."

#2745. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2742. Three Things I’m Looking At for the New York Jets vs Buffalo Bills Game

One of these days we'll have a 'Sunday fun day' as Jets fans, but it probably won't be this one. The 7-1 Bills have won four straight games and are coming to MetLife Stadium to face the 2-7 New York Jets, who have lost four straight. Jets coach Aaron Glenn claims the Jets are 'on the cusp' of miraculously winning games despite leading for 0 seconds against a Dolphins team that hadn't had a lead all season and hasn't won a game since the Jets.

All indications for this one are brutal. The Jets' current losing streak has been by an average of 9.5 points per game, while the Bills' winning streak has been by an average of 12.3 points per game. Specifically, this Jets/Bills matchup in the Aaron Glenn era is way worse: The Bills have won by an average of 23.5(!) points. The second matchup didn't even include Josh Allen; he was a healthy scratch, and they still handed the Jets a 35-8 loss.

Of course, it is any given Sunday, so if you're looking for hopeful data, the Bills are winless against the AFC East this season. Yes, it was just one game, but so far, that one divisional game is the one blemish on the 2026 season. Also, before they faced Carson Wentz and the cluster of Vikings' QBs this week, the Bills' defense was allowing 28.5 points per game. The 2026 Jets showed they can compete in shootouts; is this the week they finally win a shootout? Probably not.

But there is a chance! And for that, let's discuss what to look for this game:

I very rarely recycle headings from the weekly previews I've been doing on various sites for the last 6 seasons, but this header is too good and too applicable to the current situation to leave it out. The Jets' biggest current problem, by far, is scoring in the red zone. What makes this so frustrating is that they have no problem getting into the red zone. In these last few games, they've been a fantastic offense, but as soon as they cross that 20-yard line, the sacks and miscues are abundant.

I mentioned heading into the Raiders game that the Jets were the only team to be in the red zone 20+ times and have fewer than 10 red zone touchdowns. At that time, that was the second-worst red zone TD percentage in the NFL. Since then, the Browns have finally reached the red zone 20+ times (23) and scored only 9 red zone touchdowns, making them the only team in the NFL with those metrics. The Jets are still second-worst, though, at a terrible 11/27 (40.7%).

This also means that in the Chiefs and Raiders games specifically, the Jets were 2-7 (28.6%) in scoring touchdowns in the red zone. This does not include the two drives where the Jets got the ball in plus territory, moved it a combined -1 yard, and kicked two field goals (1/2). This is a serious problem for this talented unit.

The revelation of the Average Separation Score from Fantasy Points Data has changed how I view this phenomenon. It's easy to blame Frank Reich because his job title is Offensive Coordinator, and I've already shown my appreciation for Geno Smith, but when the top three receivers creating separation this season all belong to the Jets and the Jets offense is still struggling, something is clearly amiss.

Michael Nania of Jets X-Factor had a great film breakdown of Omar Cooper Jr. getting open but not being targeted and gave Frank Reich credit without discrediting Geno. Allow me to be the first to seriously question Geno's vision and ability to make the right read at the right time. This isn't me saying he's making bad reads; he's clearly having a solid year with a 67.5% completion and only three picks, but could he be making better reads?

Given the film breakdowns I've seen and how open the Jets' receivers have been, it's fair to blame Geno Smith. Reich has done his job of getting guys open; can Geno hit the right guy at the right time to put points on the board? Preferably 6s instead of 3s? I'll come to his aid again in pointing out that Adonai Mitchell caught none of his seven targets and had some bad drops. Still, since this problem has been an issue all season and not just this past game, I have to go back to the one constant.

Not only is he not throwing to the open receiver, but he's taking so long to process the field that he's taking sacks in the red zone. Can Geno speed up his process, make the 'right read' over a 'good read,' and score TDs against a team that allows TDs on 63.3% of its defensive red zone trips?

Perhaps the safest week-to-week bet any of you bettors could make is the over on the opposing RB1's rushing yards against the Jets. Last week, Kenneth Walker's line was 93.5, and he dominated for 102 rushing yards and three(!) total touchdowns.

This week, James Cook's line is 91.5, and he's coming off a 107-yard performance against the Vikings. Fun fact: Cook has rushed for 75+ yards and scored a touchdown in every game besides Week 1, so I'm ready to bet the farm on 100 yards and a touchdown this Sunday. I might sprinkle some dollars on 2+ TDs as well.

When will the Jets' run defense get better? I've seen zero indication that the play calling or personnel will change in any way to plug the clear holes on this team. It was still an issue with T'Vondre Sweat on the field; his absence certainly isn't helping. Maybe they can finally bounce back next week against the struggling Chargers and the likes of Omarion Hampton? Probably not.

I have no stats for this one. This is a pure eye test that should help continue writing the story of the Aaron Glenn Era. I've learned there are still plenty of fans who still think Glenn can be part of the solution for this franchise. It's been reported that the Jets operated during the trade deadline as if this were indeed a possibility, and they're giving him every chance to prove Woody right.

The final nail in the coffin, in my opinion, would be losing the players in the locker room. Sure, last year you can blame players as "bad apples" and suggest addition by subtraction with the trades of Quinnen Williams, Sauce Gardner, Jermaine Johnson, etc. Now, these are Glenn's guys; the visible sideline frustrations of Armand Membou, Geno Smith, and Garrett Wilson are all guys who were either brought in by Glenn or extended during the Glenn era.

It is no longer about bad apples; now it's time to blame the tree. Let's see if the players Glenn has chosen remain committed to his vision, however shaky it is. We already saw a Glenn team have no fight – the entire months of December and January last year. November would be fairly early to give up, but given the sky-high percentage of fans who've already given up, I wouldn't be surprised to see the players follow suit.

I'm looking for body language, sideline interactions, and, of course, general play to see how this team's morale looks. Let's go Jets!

#2739. NBA Cup 2026: Knicks drop to last place in group play with loss to shorthanded Pacers; Spurs dominate Warriors

The 2025-26 season was a dream for the New York Knicks, from the NBA Cup to the NBA Finals. This season is shaping up differently.

The defending Cup champions fell to 0-2 in group play on Friday with a 106-104 loss to the Indiana Pacers, who are missing Tyrese Haliburton due to a left hamstring strain. That puts New York in the bottom of the East Group B standings and all but eliminates them from the in-season tournament.

No team with two losses in group play has reached the knockout rounds in the NBA Cup since its introduction in 2023.

Beyond the NBA Cup, the loss dropped the Knicks to 6-6 in the regular season, continuing a slow start for a team that brought back nearly every contributor from last year's champion.

The game was nearly another epic comeback for the Knicks, who were down by 14 late in the third quarter but had the game tied early in the fourth. The Pacers responded with a 13-2 run to reassert control and held off one last Knicks push in the final minute.

New York cut an eight-point lead to two in the final two minutes and had a chance to steal the win when an inbounds pass went wrong for the Pacers (not helped by a missed foul call by the refs), but Karl-Anthony Towns' would-be game-tying shot clanked away.

Every Pacers starter scored in double figures, with Pascal Siakam leading the team with 19 points. That plus-29 bench points was enough to counter a 33-point, 11-assist night by Jalen Brunson.

Let's just say the second marquee game of the night did not come down to the final possession.

A clash of Western Conference contenders saw the San Antonio Spurs score the first 18 points of the game against the Golden State Warriors, who are playing without Stephen Curry.

It honestly didn't get much better from there. The Spurs were up 63-33 at halftime and led by as much as 43 during a 123-85 blowout. That game-opening run broke a Spurs franchise record.

Stephon Castle led the Spurs with 32 points on 12-of-18 shooting, while Victor Wembanyama contributed 12 points, 12 rebounds, 6 assists and 2 blocks. The win gives the Spurs a commanding lead in West Group C.

#2738. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2737. Falcons Draft 2027: Eight Prospects to Watch for Week 11 of College Football

Somehow, it's already Week 11 of college football in one of the craziest college football seasons we've seen in recent memory. If you're an Atlanta Falcons fan, this may be one of the first weeks you're actually reading this, and if so, welcome in! If not, I apologize for not being here last week; I had some really bad internet issues. But we're back, and with an abbreviated slate of games this week, there are only eight 2027 NFL Draft prospects that I want to focus on for the Falcons and their fans.

Before you leave, I promise there will be plenty of big men that we are going to talk about, but I wanted to make sure we discussed one of the better risers in the receiver market for the 2027 draft. Carlos Hernandez is seventh in the nation in receiving yards with 945 and is third in the ACC, behind only Malachi Toney and another 2027 riser, Umari Hatcher.

Hernandez is a three-level winner and one of the more explosive receivers with great ability with the ball in his hands. If Jahan Dotson or Zachariah Branch doesn't push the needle down the field, Hernandez can become that guy who can open up the offense in the future.

I will not allow us to forget that there is a potential left tackle of the future down the road in Athens. Earnest Greene III has all the nastiness that you want from a run-first tackle and enough in his kick-step and long enough arms (35") to hold his own in pass protection. He also signals the movement toward more size that seems to be happening in Atlanta with his 6'5", 340-pound frame. Let's see if the Outland Trophy Player of the Week from last week can follow it up with another test against Daeden Hopkins, who has been quiet since his 3.5-sack day against Mississippi State, and Darris Smith, who has some freaky traits to watch.

The NFC South is a weird division, and the Falcons, especially this season, are a weird team. Which means that there is a slim chance that this Falcons team overachieves down the stretch and falls out of the top ten in the draft. If they do, and they still want to find a great offensive tackle, USC's Elijah Paige is subject to fall a bit due to the sheer amount of talent at the position in this draft. Paige is a fluid mover and, while he's not as wide, his 6'7", 320-pound frame is more than imposing enough, especially when he's on the move. He's a violent run blocker and has a chance to showcase that against the Indiana defense, which has the 7th-best rushing success rate against the run allowed, according to GameOnPaper. Indiana also employs the Big Ten sack leader in Tobi Osunsanmi, which should make for a great matchup.

When building a culture, there is something to say about the attitude of the players that you bring in. Lance Heard brings some of the nastiest attitude you can find in an offensive tackle, and he's going to need every bit of that nastiness against Florida and their twitched-up edge rusher Emmanuel Oyebadejo.

Trading Jesse Bates hurts; there's no doubt about that. However, if there was any draft class to find a potential long-term safety, it's the 2027 class. One of the players who may find themselves falling due to the number of names in this class, similar to the Paige conversation, is LSU safety Ty Benefield. At 6'3", 216 pounds, Benefield is a massive defensive back who has truly special versatility both near the line of scrimmage and ranging in coverage. While Xavier Watts is ranging, Benefield can cause pure havoc underneath. Against Arch Manning, Ryan Wingo and a returning Cam Coleman, Benefield has a chance to really show out against Texas.

The Falcons need cornerback help in the worst possible way. While I will admit, I would hope for a cornerback that is a bit bigger than 6'1", 190-pound Vicari Swain, but his talent is too much to ignore. Swain has awesome speed and acceleration that allow him to not only be a great cornerback, but also an amazing returner as well, which can give the Falcons a future kick return duo of Swain and Branch, if Branch can develop into the type of returner we believe he can be in the future. Also, despite the quarterback play for the Razorbacks, Swain has a fun matchup against 6'2, 224-pound Chris Marshall.

Speed. To. Power. Anthony Holmes, Jr. is one of those guys where you look and say, "300 pounds shouldn't be able to move like that. As Texas Tech ramps up for another potential CFB playoff run, they have to get over Drew Mestemaker, Justin Bowick, and the Oklahoma State Cowboys offense, which has one of the better interior offensive linemen in the country in center Tyler Mercer and right guard Joseph Hanson.

I think Zereoue Williams may be the biggest riser in this season's NFL Draft, period. His extremely active feet and hands, along with his strength and flexibility, just make him the perfect offensive tackle prospect. He'll need every inch of that flexibility going up against the explosive Chase Kennedy and having to keep up with this explosive Arizona offensive attack, headlined by the combination of Noah Fifita and Isaiah Mizell.

#2734. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2731. Rapid Recap: Magic 107, Bucks 99

The Milwaukee Bucks fall to 0/2 in the NBA Cup and 1-12 overall, losing to the Orlando Magic 107-99 in a game that was at least competitive. Jaime Jaquez Jr. led the Bucks yet again with 22 points off the bench on 8/15 shooting and 5/7 from three. Franz Wagner starred for the Magic, dropping a near triple-double of 35 points, 12 assists, and nine rebounds (at one point it was a triple-double, but they apparently retracted a rebound).

With Ousmane Dieng getting the surprise start, the Bucks began slowly, with Myles Turner turning the ball over twice in the first two possessions and the team going 1/5 from the field and 1/4 from the charity stripe as the Magic led 12-4 at the seven-minute mark. Milwaukee responded, however, with a 10-3 run, thanks in large part to Kel'el Ware, who impacted early with a put-back slam and a corner triple. We even got treated to first-quarter Gary Trent Jr. minutes, but his elite defence couldn't stop Franz Wagner, who carved up the visitors in the pick-and-roll as the Magic took a 23-15 lead into the first major break.

Wagner continued to kill the Bucks early in the second, nailing two threes to extend Orlando's lead to 13 just over two minutes in as Milwaukee's shooting woes continued. Thankfully, a select few Bucks were shooting well, including Myles Turner, who nailed his third three of the night to cut the deficit to 10 with seven minutes to play. After starting 0/5, Tyler Herro began to cook as the second wore on, hitting a three, a floater, and a few free throws as the Bucks inched closer, down 34-28 halfway through the period. Unfortunately, the Magic closed on an 18-8 run as their balanced attack dominated the Bucks on both ends, leading 52-36 at the half.

Coach Jenkins started Nate Ament in Dieng's place to start the third, and the rookie paid that faith off, opening the quarter with a corner three after going 1/6 in the first half. In general, the Bucks looked much more fluid on offence, but they couldn't get stops as Desmond Bane punished them twice from deep to give his side a 65-45 edge four minutes in. The visitors wouldn't give in, though; they knuckled down on defence, which allowed them to play more in transition and go on a 10-0 run. That said, the Bucks had major free throw issues, making just 7/15 (!) midway through the third. That's when Bane really started to cook, knocking in a three plus the foul, then another three on the next possession as the margin blew back out to 16. Jaime Jaquez Jr. was huge to close the quarter, though, nailing multiple bombs and a mid-range shot to keep the Bucks within shouting distance, down 84-72 after three.

It was a frantic start to the fourth, with Milwaukee giving up plenty of open looks but the Magic failing to hit any; as a result, the visitors kept their deficit around 10 as they lived at the line. The Magic's lead stayed around that margin for the next four minutes as the Bucks' parade to the charity stripe continued (though they kept missing when they got there). In fact, Milwaukee didn't hit a field goal for over six minutes, but remarkably, they remained (somewhat) in the game. In the end, though, Wagner was too much, leading his side to their first home victory of the season.

The Bucks were atrocious from the free throw line, making just 18 of 31 attempts in a game they lost by eight. Kyle Kuzma shot a staggering 7/15 on his own!

I physically cannot watch Kuzma-ball anymore, man. It's buyout time (even though I get that he has no incentive to want that).

#2730. Colts activate Arden Key from injured reserve, place two veterans on IR

The Indianapolis Colts announced several roster moves Saturday ahead of their Week 10 matchup against the Miami Dolphins, activating defensive end Arden Key from injured reserve, signing linebacker West Weeks to the 53-man roster and placing tight end Mo Alie-Cox and linebacker Akeem Davis-Gaither on injured reserve.

The return of Key provides some much-needed help along Indianapolis' defensive line, particularly following the recent trade of DeForest Buckner to the San Francisco 49ers. The 30-year-old defensive end has been sidelined since suffering a hamstring injury during the Colts' Week 4 victory over the Washington Commanders in London, forcing him to miss the team's past five games. Before the injury, Key had started all four contests, recording 8 tackles, half a sack and 1 forced fumble.

Key returned to practice Wednesday and was a full participant throughout the week, although he was listed as questionable on Friday's injury report. His activation makes him eligible to return Sunday, potentially strengthening a pass-rushing rotation that has relied heavily on Laiatu Latu, Jaylahn Tuimoloau and Caden Curry.

Unfortunately, the Colts also received some disappointing injury news, with Alie-Cox and Davis-Gaither both landing on injured reserve.

Davis-Gaither, meanwhile, has been sidelined by a knee injury and also missed last Sunday's game against the Dallas Cowboys. His placement on injured reserve further weakens an already thin linebacker group, particularly with Jaylon Carlies also ruled out for Sunday's contest. Rookie CJ Allen and Bryce Boettcher are expected to handle significant responsibilities, while the promotion of West Weeks provides some additional depth alongside Austin Ajiake.

Weeks, an undrafted rookie out of LSU, joined Indianapolis during the offseason and has spent the season developing on the practice squad. His promotion gives the Colts another option at linebacker and on special teams as they attempt to navigate their growing injury concerns.

With Indianapolis sitting at 2-7 and having lost five consecutive games, the organization is increasingly focused on evaluating younger talent over the remainder of the season. Key's return is a welcome development for the defensive line, but the latest injuries create additional challenges for a roster that has already undergone substantial changes in recent weeks.

#2729. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2720. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

#2718. Knicks’ valiant comeback against Pacers falls short with NBA Cup reign likely kaput

INDIANAPOLIS — About a year prior, Jalen Brunson nailed a stepback, game-winning 3-pointer in the final seconds against the Pacers.

On Friday night inside the same Gainbridge Fieldhouse, a nearly identical situation presented itself, but coach Mike Brown instead called on Karl-Anthony Towns. Captain Clutch was a decoy on the other side of the court. The result was a fadeaway miss off the rim from KAT, a 106-104 Knicks defeat and probable end to New York's NBA Cup defense.

"I thought it was a great shot. I was excited for the opportunity," Towns told The Post. "I got to make a quick decision. It's either OG is open on the rip screen and he has an open layup or I get to take the shot. I just was ready for the opportunity. … I'm happy with the follow through. I was happy with the technique. It just wasn't my day."

Karl-Anthony Towns (32) of the New York Knicks handles the ball against the Indiana Pacers in Indianapolis on Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Knicks launched an improbable comeback to reach that dramatic conclusion, recovering from an eight-point deficit in the final 90 seconds because Indiana kept coughing up possessions.

With 2.8 seconds remaining, the Pacers threw away an inbounds pass — largely because of Mikal Bridges' hounding defense — which set up KAT's opportunity.

Jalen Brunson of the New York Knicks plays defense during a game against the Indiana Pacers in Indianapolis on Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"We needed a 2-pointer. We had executed something like that in practice," Brown said. "We thought we would gain an advantage with a little rip screen. KAT got a great look. Probably a shot that he makes nine out of 10 times. So I'm okay with it.

"Karl-Anthony Towns' big chance bounced off the rim."

Technically, the Knicks aren't eliminated from the NBA Cup with an 0-2 record. It's still theoretically possible to win the group or a wild-card spot with a 2-2 record. But in the NBA Cup's three-year history, no team has advanced with anything less than a 3-1 mark in the group phase.

The Knicks are the lone squad in their five-team group without a win.

New York Knicks center Karl-Anthony Towns (32) reacts to a call against the Indiana Pacers in Indianapolis on Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

There's still much more time to defend their bigger championship, but the Knicks (6-6) hardly have looked like the juggernaut of their magical run of May and June.

They missed 30 3-pointers and gave up 17 offensive rebounds in Indiana, the place of several Knicks heartbreaks over the past few years.

Brunson dropped 33 points with 11 assists — including a powerful third-quarter burst after being motivated by taunts from Pacers defender Quenton Jackson — but Captain Clutch went cold in the fourth quarter and wasn't involved in the final play.

New York Knicks guard Jalen Brunson (11) shoots as Indiana Pacers guard Andrew Nembhard defends in Indianapolis on Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Towns finished with 23 points and 15 rebounds, breaking a 56-year franchise record with his 12th double-double to start a season. He eclipsed Willis Reed's mark from 1970 and then came up just a little short with an opportunity to extend New York's NBA Cup hopes.

"That play was something we liked, and we went to it. But I'll say this: That play did not cost us the game," Brown said. "We were lucky a little bit, and our guys did fight to give us a chance down the stretch. But I don't care who you play, it's going to be hard to win a game on the road when you lose the possession game like we did, if we get blown by on defense as bad as we did, if you let your opponent win the physicality battle like we did. It's all a recipe for a loss. And that's where I'm disappointed."
