#2717. Rams Reacts: Why is fan confidence fractured?

The Los Angeles Rams have won three straight games, but fans are not ready to believe that the team is headed in the right direction again until they beat somebody good and do it with gusto. The Rams have beaten the Chargers, Raiders, and Commanders, but over 58% of fans in our SB Nation Reacts survey are not confident that this team is headed in the right direction.

Puka Nacua going on IR after the latest L.A. win likely dipped fans faith that the team will keep up in a crowded NFC. Don't go looking on FanDuel for any prop bets for Nacua, he's out for at least the next four games.

Though, Nacua being out could open the door for other player props on FanDuel like CJ Daniels, and Konata Mumpfield. Could it be time for them or another Ram to breakout?

Still though, the Rams have won three games over the last three weeks, and raised their record to 5-4. You can argue that confidence in this team should be higher than roughly 40%. However, confidence among Rams' fans is mostly split and leans negative as they enter Week 10.

When the 2026 season started, the Rams were favorites to win the Super Bowl, and if you made that bet on FanDuel, you may be regretting it now. Though, it's never too late to pick another Super Bowl favorite or underdog right at FanDuel.

In Week 1, the Rams got mauled by the San Francisco 49ers. Since then, they've lost three other times including an embarrassing Week 6 loss to the Arizona Cardinals. That loss was arguably the lowest point of the season for both the team and their fans.

Their other losses include a Week 3 blown 16-point lead to the Denver Broncos on Sunday Night Football, and a Week 5 Monday night game where they couldn't close out the Buffalo Bills.

What's the Rams most defeating loss of the season? It's up for debate. Regardless, each loss has shaken the fans and their confidence that L.A. is headed in the right direction. Fan confidence surged after they beat the New York Giants in Week 2, and that's the highest it's been since, as told by the chart below.

Confidence isn't necessarily "low" among fans, but it is fractured. Myles Garrett's (would you bet on Garrett to win Defensive Player of the Year?) return certainly has fans optimistic for the defense, but the offense is a massive question mark with both Nacua and Davante Adams out. Should the Rams have made a trade to address their WR room? Too late now.

The special teams have been a question mark all year, but credit Brandon McManus who's been perfect for the Rams, so far, while Joshua Karty is adding an element with his "dirty kicks" on kickoffs.

Both these statements can be true: At 5-4, there are qualities to like about this Rams team, and if you look at the number of players on IR, and consider their losses, there is plenty to be concerned about too.

A win over the Cardinals in Week 10 may not move the needle much, though a loss would be devastating. But if the Rams can beat the Cardinals, and follow that up with a win over the Green Bay Packers in Week 12 they'd be on a 5-game win streak. That streak may put the fan confidence meter at the highest it's been since Week 2, and potentially the Rams firmly in the playoff picture.

#2715. Juice your growth and save on taxes in this workplace investment account

Workers are in the heart of open enrollment season for next year's benefits, and rising healthcare costs are shining a spotlight on an offering that includes a tax-advantaged investment account.

Healthcare expenses are climbing for workers and employers alike, with average total family premiums, including the employer's share, averaging $28,303, up 5% from a year ago, according to data from health policy organization KFF, formerly the Kaiser Family Foundation.

To manage the costs, a growing number of employers are moving to high-deductible health plans, which work alongside health savings accounts or HSAs. Almost 40% of companies with at least 10 employees that offer health benefits also have an HSA-qualified plan, KFF found.

Health savings accounts have three major tax advantages. First, contributions that your employer automatically deducts from your pay aren't taxed. Second, money stashed into the HSA grows free of taxes. Finally, you can make tax-free withdrawals for qualified health expenses – just be sure to save your receipts.

"I love HSAs because of their triple-tax advantage," said Eileen Olson, CFP and founder of Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina, near Savannah, Georgia. "For people who can afford to leave it alone, it can be one of the best retirement accounts they're going to have."

In 2027, those with individual healthcare coverage through a high-deductible plan can stash up to $4,500 in an HSA, including any contributions from employers. That number goes up to $9,000 for family plans. Individuals can kick in a catch-up contribution of $1,000 if they're aged 55 and older and not enrolled in Medicare.

The HSA differs from the healthcare flexible spending account, known as the FSA.

FSA funds must be used by the end of the plan year unless your employer allows a carryover of up to $700 or an additional 2.5 months to use the money. HSAs, however, have no expiration date on their funds – and that's what makes them such a powerful tool for long-term planning. You can also take the HSA with you if you change employers.

"Get the money deployed, take advantage of the pretax contribution, and allocate the money into a growth portfolio," said Mark Stancato, certified financial planner at VIP Wealth Advisors in Decatur, Georgia. "With the HSA, you can keep compounding year over year."

The structure of an HSA and the available investment menu will vary from one plan to the next, and some will require that employees keep some of their holdings in cash.

Preset HSA fund menus tend to have 15 to 25 funds to choose from, and providers say that accountholders who invest tend to use two to four funds, according to Greg Carlson, senior research analyst for equity strategies at Morningstar.

How you allocate your HSA will depend on several factors, including your family's health prospects in the next year, your cash flow needs – how much you can comfortably sock into the HSA – and your risk tolerance, said André Small, CFP and founder of A Small Investment in Houston.

Small's standard is to keep an amount equal to 120% of the plan's out-of-pocket maximum held in cash in the HSA.

"That covers a bad year without forcing a sale at the wrong time," he said. "Above that amount? I typically advise investing the amount the client is comfortable investing, allocated to their risk tolerance, their time horizon and the goal of spending these dollars on care later."

For 2027, the IRS defines a high-deductible plan as one with an annual deductible no less than $1,750 for self-only coverage ($3,500 for family plans) and with an out-of-pocket expense limit that doesn't exceed $8,700 for self-only (or $17,400 for family coverage). Out-of-pocket expenses include deductibles, copays and other costs – but not premiums.

Other advisors suggest having up to the deductible in cash, though some HSA plan providers require a stated minimum amount in cash.

Beyond this cash reserve, financial advisors recommend allocating into stocks.

Patrick Swift, CFP and president of Amplius Wealth Advisors in Blue Bell, Pennsylvania, aims for an allocation of up to 60% in U.S. large cap stocks, 20% in small and mid-cap stocks and 20% in international stocks.

"We believe the equity portfolio should be well diversified," he said. "If the [HSA] balance is 2% to 10% of your net worth, why not be aggressive? That can change based on the situation, but if you are comfortable with the philosophical aspect, why not be growth oriented?"

Similarly, Stancato likes to go heavy on equity exposure in HSA accounts. "I allocate more dollars into small caps and emerging markets – those asset classes with more volatility but also more opportunity for growth," the advisor said. He also adds some large-cap domestic exposure.

For the adventurous, select HSA providers are offering brokerage window access, meaning investors can pick from a broader array of investments, including individual stocks.

This year, seven providers surveyed by Morningstar offer access to brokerage windows – up from four in 2023 – including HealthEquity, Fidelity and Lively. Be aware that there may be annual fees attached, either a flat dollar amount or a percentage of assets.

Rachel Elson, a CFP and wealth advisor at Perigon Wealth Management in San Francisco, recommends keeping things simple. "You can do a three-ETF model, covering some allocation that includes the U.S. market, and two ETFs for developed and emerging markets."

"You don't need to get super complicated here," she added.

#2711. One stat shows how Ben Johnson and the Bears are helping Caleb Williams thrive

As rough as Caleb Williams' night was against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers last week, those struggles don't take away from the incredible progress the Chicago Bears quarterback has made in his third NFL season.

And as many of us predicted, the jump in his performance has less to do with an increase in the spectacular as it does with his growing mastery of the ordinary.

A recent ESPN analysis of each NFL team's report card through the first half of the season highlighted just how much Williams has grown for the Bears in a particularly key stat.

"Williams leads the NFL in completion percentage (82.8%) when not pressured. He is ahead of the pace for the highest single-season completion percentage in that situation by a season-qualifying quarterback since ESPN first tracked pressures in 2009," the article lays out.

Naturally, you'd expect Williams and most quarterbacks to play better when they're not pressured. But comparing Williams to himself over the last few years is pretty mindblowing.

His passing grade (84.6), adjusted completion percentage (83.7), and passer rating (106.4) when kept clean are all the highest of his career, according to Pro Football Focus. The adjusted completion percentage, in particular, is wildly better than his first two seasons, highlighting his increased ability to hit the easy stuff.

It's also a credit to his coaching and the protection in front of him.

Johnson has been in his bag this season, routinely alternating between opening up the middle of the field, creating quick, simple third-down throws, and using the screen game as an extension of the run game to get the ball into playmakers' hands. Last week's four-INT game notwithstanding, Williams has done a masterful job of keeping the ball out of harm's way and taking what the defense gives him.

His offensive line has generally held up its end, too, despite facing a revolving door at left tackle due to injuries. Through six games, the Bears offensive line (according to PFF) has been responsible for relatively few pressures compared to other quarterbacks, and Williams (albeit in a smaller sample size) has only been taken down 14 times this year—tied for 21st in the league. It's not easy protecting for an average of 3.33 seconds each play—the average amount of time Williams takes before throwing. But they're collectively making it work.

Given how hard the Bucs made things for Williams, the rest of the Bears' opponents will not doubt try to duplicate Tampa Bay's game plan and force Williams into tough situations. But as long as he continues to make the right plays, especially when he's kept clean, he and the Bears will be just fine.

Added Holland regarding Ryan: “He decided he wanted to turn and didn’t care, and just ran over both of my feet, my wife’s foot and got her leg too.”

“He was told in no uncertain terms what he had done, and instead of staying at the scene or even saying, ‘Sorry,’ he just got back in his vehicle and took off,” Holland said.

Holland gave the Post a photo of his wife’s injuries.

“I mean, just another inch or two, and his mirror or bumper would have taken off [my wife’s] knee,” Holland said. “His tire literally was like so close to her that it scraped her leg and ran over her feet and both of my feet. Holland took a photo of Ryan’s red Ford Truck, with a Jets license plate holder and a vanity tag that says “HIT QBS.”

#2708. Pacers 106, Knicks 104: “I’m sure Indiana will look like an all star team tonight.”

Daltong called it. "I'm sure Indiana will look like an all star team tonight." The Hoosiers may have entered the game 4–8 overall and 1–4 at home, but even while weathering the absence of Tyrese Haliburton, they have another gear available to them. For instance, on Veterans Day, when they ripped open the fourth quarter with 14 unanswered points. Or tonight, when they outscored the Knicks (6–6) by 13 early in the final frame and held on for a 106–104 win.

Tonight's tilt at Gainbridge Fieldhouse was another Group B game in the NBA Cup tourney. The Knicks were favored because they're the more talented team on paper, but their effort showed up too late to steal the win. They were simply out-hustled for most of the game. In the positive column, Karl-Anthony Towns registered his twelfth straight double-double (23 PTS, 15 RBS), breaking Willis Reed's franchise record for consecutive double-doubles to start a season. And Mikal Bridges took 12 shots! For 18 points! On the less positive side, Josh Hart went scoreless on four shots, the Knicks lost the paint by 20 and second-chance points by nine, and yet again they attempted double-digit fewer free throws than their opponent (IND 18-of-25, NYK 10-of-13).

Shield your eyes from all this yellow . . . looks a little like a filthy urinal.

The game started inelegantly, with Andrew Nembhard (12 PTS, 4-14 FG, 6 AST, 6 TO) taking the opening tip in for an easy layup. At the other end, Hart thought KAT was going to cut. Instead, KAT crowded Jalen Brunson (33 PTS, 11-22 FG, 11 AST, +8) along the arc, and Nembhard deflected Hart's pass out of bounds. That kind of confusion is predictable this early in the season, especially with Hart having missed a few games already. So is KAT's ability to steamroll Ivica Zubac (12 PTS, 13 RBS) for snacks at the cup.

OG Anunoby (11 PTS, 4-13 FG, +8) laced an early triple when Hart noticed that Pascal Siakam (19 PTS, 5-13 FG) was napping in transition. OG and Pascal have a history, and they made each other's jobs difficult for most of the night. (After that three, OG would make one of his next seven attempts.)

The Knicks took control with a 9–0 run that turned a 9–9 tie into an 18–9 lead. Towns scored seven of those nine and finished the quarter with 12 points and three rebounds. The oft-maligned Bridges chipped in six points and a steal (welcome back!), and Hart had four boards and three dimes. Brunson scored just two points but set up three baskets before picking up his second foul, an offensive foul, and heading to the bench.

Jay Huff brought Indiana back with eight quick points off the bench, including two threes and an alley-oop from that pest T.J. McConnell. Miles McBride answered with a longball, but the Knicks went more than three minutes without scoring after that. Deuce missed his next three attempts, Shamet bricked a couple, and Huff blocked Andre Drummond at the rim. Indiana went on a 15–5 run, taking a 24–23 lead on an OAKAAKUYOAK Obi Toppin bank shot. Towns returned and restored the lead with a driving and-one. The good guys were up 27–26 at the horn.

If you enjoyed the stanky shooting and one-and-dones of the first quarter, the Knicks cordially invited you to more of the same in the second. OG tied it at 31 on another Towns-as-hub laser, then Indy rattled off a 13–4 run. Toppin laced a three and finished a fast break, while Zubac elbowed his way to three O-boards on one possession before putting it in. No Diawara tonight, but Pacôme Dadiet made a brief (as in one minute) appearance in the second.

Yet again, free throws were the biggest bugaboo. In their last game, the Knicks shot 14 fewer free throws than Cleveland. In tonight's second quarter, Indiana went 13-of-17 while New York shot . . . 1-of-2. Call the vet! These zebras have rabies!

Brunson scored half of the team's 22 second-quarter points. Towns grabbed six rebounds but scored just two points, and Josh Hart, Landry Shamet, and Miles McBride all fired blanks. Aaron Nesmith scored six straight, the last on a technical free throw after Mike Brown drew a tech, to stretch Indiana's lead to 14. Brunson hit a turnaround and fed Tyler Kolek for a late three to regain some ground. Indy led 58–49 at the break.

Through the first half, our heroes were dishing the ball fine, with seven more assists than the Hoosiers. Rebounds and points in the paint were basically a dead heat. The voodoo was in the fouls. The Knicks had committed twice as many as the Pacers (14–7). And somehow Rick Carlisle found it necessary to complain about the calls.

On to the third. Cap was cooking, with 16 points and four dimes. The Knicks needed it all and then some. Brunson led an 8–0 run that brought New York within five, but the comeback stalled before they could get back to black. Kelly Oubre Jr. and Nesmith each scored seven in the period, Zubac added six points and five boards, and the hosts were up by 14 again.

From there, the Knicks closed on a 15–2 run to get within a point. McBride hit two bombs, Shamet added one, and Drummond grabbed three defensive boards and a steal. Deuce and Anunoby both missed chances to take the lead in the final eight seconds, so the Knicks went into the fourth trailing 85–84.

From there, two scenarios were possible. Either the Champs would maintain their momentum and turn back those pesky Pacers, or Indy would do what they did against the Wiz on Wednesday and go on some crazy run. 'Twas the latter, fans. After Anunoby tied it at 87, the hosts answered with a 15–2 run. Nembhard scored six points in the quarter, Oubre added five points and two steals, and Zubac finished with nine offensive boards. The Knicks kept fumbling and bumbling the ball away. This time Captain Clutch couldn't save them either, managing only four points in the fourth after scoring 16 in the third.

Behold the bumbling and fumbling of which I wrote:

New York still had a shot to tie it in the final minute. Brunson's layup with 26 seconds left cut the deficit to four, and after Siakam missed both free throws, Bridges (!) hit a pull-up to make it 106–104. Anunoby picked off Siakam's pass with three seconds left, but Towns missed a 15-foot turnaround and the Pacers held on. Ballgame.

If you're feeling testy after another loss, so is Jose Alvarado. Evidently, he and Quenton Jackson have prior history of physical scrapes (including an altercation when Alvarado was still with the Pelicans). Jackson not only taunted Jalen Brunson tonight, but in the third quarter he jawed at Rick Brunson, too. Jose might be out of the rotation, but the big dawg is always ready to defend his captain!

Up to Toronto our heroes go to play the Raps on Sunday. This is your recap, chaps. Safe travels, Knickerbockers.

#2707. Saints passing attack has advantage in matchup versus Panthers

Week 10 includes a battle for first place in the NFC South with the New Orleans Saints hosting the Carolina Panthers. The Saints and Panthers are both 3-5, with the winner grabbing sole possession of the division lead. New Orleans has won two of their last three games, while the Panthers are in the midst of a three-game losing streak.

Most of the success that the Saints have had offensively has been because of quarterback Tyler Shough and his receivers. However, New Orleans has gotten off to excruciatingly bad starts in every game this year. They'll be facing a Carolina defense that ranks 21st overall but has fared better statistically against the pass. It's there that we focus this analysis of Sunday's clash.

▪︎ 211.5 yards per game (9th)▪︎ 63.3% completion percentage▪︎ 12 touchdowns (16th)▪︎ 5 interceptions (20th)▪︎ 11 sacks▪︎ 32 QB hits

If not for 406 passing yards given up to Detroit in Week 4, the Panthers would rank even higher against the pass. Four of Carolina's other seven opponents have been held to less than 200 net yards through the air. However, the Packers and Broncos have both put up over 220 passing yards against the Panthers in the last two weeks. This can be mostly attributed to mounting injuries in the team's secondary.

Carolina has been without starting cornerbacks Jaycee Horn and Mike Jackson for five games each, greatly thinning their coverage unit. The good news for the Panthers is that Jackson (groin) was a full participant in practice by the end of this week and could be activated off injured reserve to play against the Saints. Rookie fourth-round choice Will Lee (knee) also was back at practice in full capacity and likely resumes his starting duties. Lee's 7 pass breakups lead the Panthers, but practice squad player Corey Thornton is the only Panthers corner to have an interception. Undersized Chau Smith-Wade and rookie Malcolm DeWalt IV round out the team's current corner crew.

Carolina does have two physical safeties in Tre'von Moehrig and Nick Scott with two solid backups in Lathan Ransom and Zakee Wheatley. Scott, the usual free safety, has by far the most range. Wheatley has probably the best ball skills of the four and has an interception with four passes broken up. At linebacker, Devin Lloyd and Brian Okereke are not only Carolina's leading tacklers but also strong in coverage. The athletic Lloyd has a team-high two interceptions, returning one for a touchdown, with him and Okereke (one interception) combining to break up nine opposing throws, but Okereke (foot) will be sidelined against the Saints.

Carolina was one of the league's worst at pressuring the quarterback last year. Despite a handful of new additions, the same trend has continued this season. Edge Princely Umanmielen, a 2025 third-round pick, leads the Panthers with just 3 sacks. Pricey free-agent acquisition Jaelan Phillips has contributed 2.5 sacks and a team-best 6 QB hits. Both are capable of much more disruption, which the Panthers are counting on for their stretch run. Carolina is also capable of disruption on the inside, but hasn't gotten it from Derrick Brown, Lee Hunter, Tershawn Wharton, or Bobby Brown III. The Panthers' down linemen have yet to record a sack this season, with the group combining for just six QB hits.

▪︎ 230.5 yards per game (14th)▪︎ 66.2% completion percentage▪︎ 14 touchdowns (12th)▪︎ 6 interceptions (16th)▪︎ 20 sacks

Second-year quarterback Tyler Shough has been under tremendous pressure all year thanks to a porous offensive line and the absence of a competent running game. Despite this, Shough has been productive on a fairly consistent basis and particularly clutch in critical situations. Dealing with an injured non-throwing hand, Shough has shown both poise and accuracy from the pocket and the ability to escape the rush to make plays with his legs or deliver with pinpoint precision on the move.

Shough's favorite target is wideout Chris Olave, who is well on his way to the best numbers of his five-year career. Olave has caught 56 of his 83 targets for 734 yards and two touchdowns. Those impressive numbers are only part of the story. Olave's route precision and ability to quickly diagnose coverages allow consistent production in spite of heavy defensive attention.

Wideout Devaughn Vele has been a terrific complement to Olave, particularly on third downs or key situations. Vele has 35 receptions for 365 yards and three scores, with 19 of his catches resulting in a first down. Shough's trust in Vele is clear, with the receiver often converting with acrobatic catches in traffic. Tight end Juwan Johnson is another security blanket for Shough. Though he's cooled after a hot start, Johnson has 28 catches for 289 yards and a team-best four touchdowns while providing a huge matchup problem for most defenses.

New Orleans has young depth at wideout. Rookies Barion Brown and Bryce Lance have been involved and have flashed some playmaking skills, but not on a consistent enough basis. Rookie first-round pick Jordyn Tyson got his first NFL action against the Browns last week. Tyson immediately showed why he was a top-10 pick with impressive routes and athleticism. Look for him to be a bigger part of the offense with each week.

Alvin Kamara has not been productive as a runner, but still has lethal value as a pass catcher. The return of Travis Etienne (hamstring) from injured reserve will not only boost the running game, but also give Shough another legitimate receiving weapon out of the backfield. The Saints also have two good pass catchers at tight end to back up Johnson with Noah Fant and rookie Oscar Delp. Neither has been a big contributor as a pass catcher, combining for 16 catches and 116 yards. However, both have been used as extra pass protectors recently (as have the running backs) and each is a capable receiver at all three levels.

New Orleans pass protection got a huge boost with the return of left tackle Kelvin Banks Jr. from injured reserve last week. Banks and left guard David Edwards make a formidable duo on that side, but there are major questions about the middle and right side of the Saints line. Center Erik McCoy also sustained a biceps injury in that same game and will be sidelined for a minimum of a few weeks. At least initially, right guard Cesar Ruiz will slide into McCoy's spot at center, with either William Sherman or rookie Jeremiah Wright stepping in at guard. Right tackle Taliese Fuaga (knee) will also be sidelined against Carolina, with his spot taken by either Asim Richards or rookie Mason Murphy. This is typically the spot where Derrick Brown lines up, so certainly an area of concern for New Orleans on Sunday.

Tyler Shough notched his first career victory against Carolina by throwing for 282 yards and two scores in a 2025 Week 10 matchup. Five weeks later, Shough tossed for 272 yards and a touchdown to complete the season sweep. In those two meetings, Shough has completed 72.9% of his throws for 554 yards and three touchdowns without an interception. Chris Olave has also forged All-Pro numbers against the Panthers. In eight career games against Carolina, Olave has 38 receptions for 534 yards and four scores with two 100-yard games and two more outings of over 80 yards.

#2706. FAA lifts ground stop, clearing the way for NFL teams to travel on Saturday

Friday’s sudden, and objectively concerning, decision by the FAA to issue only the second nationwide order to keep all planes from flying since 9/11 has ended.

The situation should result in no impact on NFL teams traveling to their road games for Sunday. It also should allow college teams to get to wherever they need to go for Saturday.

If it had happened one day later, the end result could have created chaos for NFL teams, significantly delaying their travel and turning their schedules for the night before the game upside down.

The situation came as a surprise to some NFL teams. For example, 49ers coach Kyle Shanahan learned about it during his Friday press conference.

Asked about the ground stop impacting the team’s plans for travel to Dallas on Saturday based on the ground stop, Shanahan said, “When did they do that?”

Eventually, Shanahan said, “I didn’t know. I just first heard.”

The 49ers plan to leave San Francisco at 1:00 p.m. PT on Saturday. Ten other teams will be traveling on Saturday, too.

#2704. Hawks can’t keep up with Bulls in OT, lose 125-117

#2703. At least 50 different quarterbacks will have started games through Week 10

Raiders quarterback Fernando Mendoza will make his first career start on Sunday. Two others will make their debuts, too: Cardinals quarterback Carson Beck and Dolphins quarterback Kyle McCord.

Via NBC Sports research, they’ll become the 48th, 49th, and 50th different quarterbacks to start games during the 2026 season.

It will be only the second time in the last 15 years (excluding the 1987 strike season) during which 50 different quarterbacks have started games in the first 10 weeks of a season.

If, for some reason, another quarterback makes his first start of the season on Sunday (that’s unlikely with Seahawks quarterback Drew Lock not having an injury designation for Sunday), it will be the second-most starters through 10 weeks since 1950.

In 1999, 55 different quarterbacks started games through the first 10 weeks of the season.

Most of the changes have arisen from injury. For the Raiders, the transition to Mendoza happened after Kirk Cousins was traded to the Vikings.

#2701. Raiders Mailbag: Could John Spytek have gotten bigger return from Minnesota Vikings in Kirk Cousins trade?

After trading starting quarterback Kirk Cousins to the Minnesota Vikings for a third-round pick ahead of Tuesday's deadline, the Las Vegas Raiders' Week 10 game against the Seattle Seahawks will forever be known as Fernando Mendoza's first regular-season start for the Raiders. Of course, that's the primary focus of this week's mailbag. Let's get to it!

A: I get that a lot of people feel like the Raiders didn't get a big enough return for Cousins, and initially, I was surprised they didn't get more, too. I felt like John Spytek could have at least gotten an escalator on the pick where, if the Vikings made the NFC Championship, Super Bowl or whatever with Cousins, the pick becomes a second-rounder.

However, all of the reporting after the fact has been that Minnesota wasn't willing to go higher than a third for Cousins. So, if he wasn't going to be the starter in Las Vegas for much longer, at least Spytek managed to get a Top 100 pick from a player who wasn't going to be on next year's roster and probably wasn't going to be contributing for much longer this year anyway.

Why wouldn't the Vikings budge? Well, this is the same organization that moved on from Cousins a few years ago because they felt like they'd never be able to make a deep playoff run with him. Now that he's older and didn't join the team until halfway through the season, I'd imagine the logic is similar. Essentially, Minnesota was willing to take a chance that the 38-year-old can salvage the campaign, but not at the cost of potentially jeopardizing the club's future, and drew a line at the third-rounder.

Q: Can you please help me understand just what this team is doing? Trading your starting QB sends a signal that you're no longer competing; you're rebuilding. If that is the case, why do you keep your best trade asset in Maxx Crosby, who could bring you higher-round draft picks? Many people feel like the Raiders won't win another game and fear for Mendoza.

A: To be honest, I don't think my answer is going to be satisfactory for you.

It's never been a secret that this is a rebuild year for the Raiders. They put a heavy emphasis on getting younger in the offseason, and it was always clear that Kirk Cousins was a bridge-gap starter until Mendoza was ready to start. The Cousins trade doesn't happen if John Spytek and Klint Kubiak don't think the latter is true. Spytek even said that the organization feels it can still win in the second half of this season with the rookie behind center.

As far as not trading Crosby, I can agree that it'd make sense to try and get picks for him. However, the coaching staff and front office seem to think what he brings as a leader is more valuable to the culture that Kubiak is trying to build than a couple of draft picks. Also, keeping Crosby ties back to Spytek and Kubiak thinking they can stay in the playoff hunt with Mendoza.

Now, if that plan falls flat and the team struggles down the stretch, maybe the strategy changes in the offseason and they look to move Crosby for picks again. But right now, the team's brass appears comfortable seeing how the rest of the campaign plays out with the No. 1 pick leading the charge.

A: I think you're on the right track here where Kubiak's game plan is going to be to get the ball out of Mendoza's hands quickly and give him some easy throws, especially early on. There might not be a worse defense to face for a quarterback's first NFL start given how many certified dudes the Seahawks have and how confusing Mike Macdonald's scheme is.

So, I think we'll see a good amount of predetermined throws via screens, RPOs and bootlegs to simplify the rookie's reads and try to neutralize Seattle's pass rush. And of course, if there's ever a game for the rushing attack to finally hit its stride, it's this one.

A: If I'm Macdonald, I'm throwing the book at Mendoza by giving him a lot of pressure looks pre-snap and changing the picture post-snap. Basically, the strategy is to try to overwhelm and confuse the rookie as much as possible so that he starts thinking too much and either holds onto the ball too long or makes a mistake that leads to a turnover.

That's why I think Kubiak should start the game with the predetermined quick throws mentioned above. Take the thinking off Fernando's plate initially and let him get into a rhythm to build some confidence before opening up the playbook later on. To use a cross-sport reference, it'd be like a jump shooter looking to get fouled early so he can get to the line and see a few balls go through the hoop before letting it rip from three.

A: I just want to start by giving you a hard time about asking a "two-part question" when there are four questions in here, LOL!

I think Brock Bowers was always expected to be the primary receiver, and the actual receivers were viewed as complementary pieces. Bowers is doing a pretty good job of filling that role, seeing as he's putting up wide receiver numbers, ranking tied for 14th in receptions (48), 10th in yards (658) and tied for sixth in touchdowns (six) among all pass-catchers, not just tight ends. And that's after missing two games. As long as he's around, the 'WR1' issue is overblown, in my opinion.

On top of that, I think Tre Tucker has been a solid No. 2 this season. He ranks in the top 25 of receivers in catches (33, tied for 25th), yards (529, 17th) and touchdowns (four, tied for 11th). It also wouldn't be surprising to me if Tucker is even more productive with Mendoza, given the latter brings more arm strength to the table than Cousins did.

Now, Tucker is an impending free agent, and it might make more sense to save money for Bowers' extension. So, wide receiver might still be an offseason need this year, but it doesn't need to be a top priority if the plan is to open up the checkbook for the "football robot from heaven".

With Bech, he's never been a great separator and has always been more reliant on winning contested catches. That was the book on him coming out of TCU, and it has shown up during his limited playing time in the NFL so far. He was/is a decent route runner; he just isn't a high-level athlete to explode out of cuts and generate a lot of separation. He can win by being physical at the top of the route, but that isn't going to result in wide-open targets.

However, Bech has only run 59 routes this season, per Pro Football Focus, and just 19 in the two games since he returned from injury. So it's too small of a sample size for me to say definitively that's been the issue this year.

A: I'd like to see Jackson Powers-Johnson get a shot at left guard, but I don't think it will happen. One of the frustrations I have with Kubiak is that he seems to be too loyal to Spencer Buford. The Athletic's Sam Warren didn't say that directly when he joined Marcus and me on Tape Don't Lie a couple of weeks ago, but Warren did mention that the coaching staff thinks higher of Burford than what his perception is outside of the building.

Also, I'm sure some people are tired of me saying this; I get the feeling JPJ won't be on the roster after this season. Unless an injury happens where he gets put back into the lineup and plays well, I think it'll be a similar situation to Tyree Wilson, where Powers-Johnson gets shopped and traded in the offseason.

A: Let's slow down here for a second. Marcus and I aren't saying Mike Washington Jr. is a bust. Last week just highlighted that he isn't ready to take over as the lead running back ahead of Ashton Jeanty, which has always been the expectation for the fourth-round pick.

I talked about this a bunch in the offseason (and I'm pretty sure Marcus did, too); Washington is more of a complementary back who is a home-run hitter rather than a bell cow due to his lack of vision and shiftiness. The Jets game opened the door for MJW to potentially take over because he did a decent job of keeping the offense on schedule, but last week was a different story. Still, he can be a good second option behind Jeanty moving forward.

The coaching staff, especially the head coach, should have input on who the team drafts. They're the ones coaching the players after all, and ignoring their opinions is how friction gets created within the organization. The general manager should be the one making the final call, but his decision should be the result of a collaborative effort with the scouting staff's and coaching staff's opinions factored in.

It's pretty common for coaches and the front office to meet during the offseason and hash out what players they want to target in the draft.

Q: How has Rob Leonard treated No. 1 receivers like Chris Olave and Garrett Wilson? Will he adjust his coverage scheme to Jaxon Smith-Njigba?

A: Against Olave, Leonard's strategy was essentially to keep Olave in front of the secondary to prevent giving up explosive plays, since he and Tyler Shough connected on a handful of deep shots in the first two weeks of the season. So, the Raiders were willing to give up any short routes to Olave.

He still ended up having over 100 yards, but there were no explosive plays. That game is his second-lowest average depth of target (ADOT) of the season at 9.3 yards, and he had his highest YAC total (32 yards) of the season, per PFF. That was a decent strategy because the Saints didn't have another receiver who could threaten the Raiders downfield, and Olave didn't burn the Raiders deep.

Against Wilson, Leonard went with a similar strategy initially. However, the cornerbacks had their worst game of the season collectively. Darien Porter started the game with poor awareness in Cover 3, losing Wilson for a 31-yard catch. On top of that, Eric Stokes got cooked by Adonai Mitchell for the majority of the game, while Jermod McCoy was beaten deep a handful of times as well.

In other words, Leonard's strategy didn't work in New York because of a lack of execution in the secondary, and the Jets had more than one pass catcher who could burn the defense.

I'd imagine the approach this Sunday will be to bracket or double team JSN as much as possible. He's too good after the catch that you can't just take away the deep shots and give him what he wants underneath. Also, there are very few corners (maybe none) who can cover Smith-Njigba consistently throughout a game, and Vegas certainly don't have someone who can do it with how the position group has been playing lately.

The good news is Seattle doesn't have another pass-catcher who really strikes fear in opposing defenses, similar to New Orleans. Cooper Kupp is still solid, but he's well past his prime at this stage.

The bad news is JSN lines up all over the place, which makes it difficult to double team him because the defense doesn't know where he'll line up until the offense breaks the huddle. Bracket coverage impacts the entire secondary because the defense is dedicating two defenders to one pass catcher. If the unit doesn't know where that pass catcher is going to be, then they have to adjust on the fly, which can lead to coverage breaks. Also, Smith-Njigba is arguably the best receiver in the game right now and can beat bracket coverage anyway.

Long story short, the Raiders are probably going to have a tough time keeping JSN in check on Sunday. Most defenses in the league do, where it's just a matter of trying to limit his damage as much as possible.

Q: Matt, rewatching the last 4 games, I noticed the Raiders are having a hard time keeping the edge and defending passes on the flat. When they blitz, they're easy to spot and defend. How much of this is Leonard's scheme, and how much is a lack of quality defensive players? It just seems players are not in good positions to properly defend the run. Also, in your opinion, will Leonard be back next year?

A: The run defense is more of a personnel issue, in my opinion. Not having a defensive tackle who can reset the line of scrimmage and/or get penetration allows the offense to dictate where the ball goes instead of the defense forcing cutbacks. That makes the linebackers' jobs more difficult because the running back has more options/rushing lanes available, so the backers can't play fast and attack downhill. Also, there are plenty of plays where the defensive tackles are getting pushed into the second level.

That's a big reason why the Raiders are struggling to stop 12 and 13 personnel runs. Two- and three-tight-end formations mean the defense has to account for more gaps, so it's harder for the coordinator to scheme up blitzes and run stunts because there are more options for where the ball can go. When you scheme something up against the run, you're guessing at where the ball is going, and the likelihood of picking wrong increases when there are more gaps to account for. It should be an educated guess based on the offense's tendacies, but it's still a guess.

As far as coverage goes, it's a twofold answer. Yes, Leonard's scheme has left the defense susceptible in the flats, especially when he blitzes. However, opposing offenses are only worried about Maxx Crosby as a pass rusher right now because no one else is winning one-on-one opportunities consistently. So, they're sliding the protection to Crosby to set up double teams, or just chip blocking him, and no one else is taking advantage of the opportunity to force the offense to adjust.

Malcolm Koonce had a good start to the season where he was beating backup-caliber tackles, but Koonce has cooled off against better competition. And the interior pass rush has been pretty much nonexistent all season.

So, Leonard has to blitz or manufacture pressure to avoid giving quarterbacks all day in the pocket. That thins out the secondary and leaves the defense susceptible to getting beaten in coverage. However, I do think his play calling has been too predictable because offenses seem to constantly have answers when he does bring pressure.

Will Rob Leonard get fired at the end of the year? I understand why Klint Kubiak hasn't done that yet because of the personnel issues I mentioned above. But Kubiak can't have a defense that ranks dead last in points per game and is getting scored on at a historic pace and not make a significant change in the offseason.

Maybe that's a massive personnel change instead of firing the coordinator, but it isn't like Kubiak and Leonard have a long history together.

Q: The Raiders have received very limited return on investment from their defensive free agency signings. Do you think paying big bucks for young players coming off their rookie deals is the right approach? Should they target a bunch of solid veterans on reasonable short-term deals?

A: In my opinion, Spytek approached free agency last offseason the right way by targeting younger players who were coming off rookie contracts. When a team is rebuilding, older veterans on short-term contracts are the type of players a general manager should stay away from.

If the team's core is young and the organization isn't expecting to be a contender right away, then older guys who are near the end of their prime and not going to be around long-term aren't worth bringing in. Instead, the roster-building strategy should be to get as many young and talented players as possible so that they can grow together and develop into a contending team for several years to come. But that doesn't come without growing pains, and that's where the Raiders are at right now.

Now, if they finish this season around the seven- to eight-win mark and just have a few holes to patch over to become a legitimate contender in 2027, that's when Spytek should pivot toward older veterans on short-term contracts in free agency. So the strategy could flip this offseason, especially if Mendoza hits the ground running. But as far as this past spring goes, I think the Raiders went about it the right way even if the early returns are underwhelming.

That'll do it for this week's mailbag. The latter will continue to publish on Thursdays.

#2700. Joe Burrow can join Peyton Manning as only QBs with 4 total TDs in 5 straight games

Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow has been racking up touchdowns recently, and if he continues to do it on Sunday night against the Steelers, he’ll join some very exclusive company in NFL history.

Burrow has had at least four touchdowns, combined rushing and passing, in each of the last four games. If Burrow has another four-touchdown game on Sunday night, he will join Peyton Manning as the only quarterbacks in NFL history with at least four combined passing and rushing touchdowns in five consecutive games.

Burrow’s last four games:Three touchdown passes and one touchdown run in a 34-17 win over the Falcons.Five touchdown passes and one touchdown run in a 43-36 win over the Titans.Four touchdown passes in a 35-13 win over the Ravens.Four touchdown passes in a 31-24 win over the Dolphins.

Manning became the first quarterback to have five straight games with four touchdowns in Weeks 8-12 of 2004 with the Colts, when he had a six-touchdown game, two five-touchdown games and two four-touchdown games. (All of Manning’s touchdowns were passing, not rushing.) Manning was named NFL MVP that season.

And Burrow is an MVP candidate with the way he’s playing now. A win over the Steelers on Sunday night would put the Bengals in first place in the AFC North, and a big game with those big stakes would give a big boost to Burrow’s MVP chances.

#2698. Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo dominate Cavaliers in 133-121 loss in Miami

The Heat's All-Star duo gave the Cavaliers fits all night, running downhill and providing a huge problem that they just didn't have the personnel to stop. The Cavaliers look to turn the page when they face the Dallas Mavericks Saturday night in Cleveland.

#2697. Saints sent Jordyn Tyson to renowned hamstring specialist at University of Wisconsin

In 2021, the NFL awarded a $4 million grant to Dr. Bryan Heiderscheit and his team at the University of Wisconsin to research new ways to prevent and treat hamstring injuries. Saints rookie wide receiver Jordyn Tyson may benefit from that research.

Mike Triplett of NewOrleans.Football reports that the Saints sent Tyson on two separate visits to the University of Wisconsin to be evaluated by Dr. Heiderscheit and his team.

Hamstring injuries have been a major problem for Tyson, who was out for three months with a hamstring injury before finally making his NFL debut last week. Tyson also missed time at Arizona State last year because of a hamstring injury, and he didn’t run at either Arizona State’s Pro Day or the NFL Scouting Combine because of his hamstring.

But there’s hope for Tyson after his evaluations in Wisconsin. Heiderscheit was able to identify a weakness with Tyson’s hamstring that he thinks can be strengthened with a specific workout regimen that Tyson has been following.

Heiderscheit previously worked with Packers wide receiver Christian Watson and helped him recover from hamstring injuries, and it worked: Watson has started every game this season and in nine games already has career-highs with 49 catches for 879 yards, and he’s leading the NFL with an average of 17.9 yards per catch.

That’s the kind of player the Saints think Tyson can be, if he can stay healthy. Heiderscheit’s treatment may be the key to Tyson staying healthy.

#2696. NFL competition committee reportedly expected to talk strategy on eliminating future trade snafus

The NFL competition committee is reportedly expected to discuss how to eliminate future trade snafus after multiple teams altered or backed out of agreed-upon deals either before or after the league's trade deadline passed on Tuesday afternoon.

With front-office executives voicing their discontent, the committee is anticipated to address the matter during the offseason, according to Saturday report from ESPN's Adam Schefter and NFL Network's Ian Rapoport.

Executives reportedly raised concerns about trade terms still being negotiable after they're agreed to.

Here's a rewind of what went down this week:

Los Angeles Chargers head coach Jim Harbaugh suggested the league look into a "loophole" he said the Denver Broncos leveraged after the deadline to coax the Chargers into taking less compensation for linebacker Daiyan Henley.

The teams had agreed to a trade that was going to send Henley to Denver, with L.A. receiving a 2027 fourth-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick in return.

But the Broncos were reportedly granted permission to revisit the terms of the deal after consulting with the NFL on Wednesday, a day after the deadline. Denver, according to a Thursday report from The Athletic, determined that its medical evaluation of the soon-to-be 27-year-old Henley didn't align with the information the Chargers put forth prior to the trade agreement.

Like any trade, the completion of this one was pending a physical. While Denver failed Henley on his physical to due to a knee issue, it still tried to rework the deal with the Chargers, per The Athletic. At that point, though, because the deadline had passed, L.A. couldn't shop around for other trade partners. So its options were to (1) accept the revised terms or (2) call it off and welcome Henley back.

"I mean, it's a medical grade. It's a pass-fail for a medical grade," Harbaugh said in his Thursday news conference. "And they told us they weren't going to pass him if it was a fourth and a fifth, which was agreed upon. They would not pass them for that. They would pass him for a fourth and a fill in the blank."

When the reporter following up inquired to confirm that the Broncos tried to downgrade the second pick in the deal — the 2028 fifth-rounder — Harbaugh said, "Correct."

Denver was still willing to take on Henley, albeit without making as big of an investment after carrying out its own medical assessment of the fourth-year defender. L.A. didn't acquiesce, and the trade fell through.

Hours before Tuesday's trade deadline, it was reported that the Cincinnati Bengals agreed a deal that would land them Philadelphia Eagles edge rusher Arnold Ebiketie. In exchange, the Bengals were reportedly poised to give up a sixth-round pick.

But, really, the agreed-upon trade was going to be more costly for Cincinnati due to the compensatory-pick formula. Essentially, because the Eagles signed Ebiketie as a compensatory free agent this past offseason, the Bengals would actually forfeit a third-round compensatory pick if the trade went through.

This potential problem was flagged on social media by analysts before the trade ultimately broke down.

The Bengals backed out from the trade, leaving the selling Eagles hanging. That was a bad look for Cincinnati, and the optics only worsened when director of player personnel Duke Tobin sent head coach Zac Taylor out to explain the situation on Wednesday.

"Obviously, we tried something yesterday," Taylor said. "Duke was very explicit with me when he came and spoke to me of how he wanted this portrayed. I'm not comfortable doing it, but I'm going to do what he asked.

"We did make an effort to kind of put some depth in there in place of Cashius [Howell]. As we got into it, obviously it was going to be a little more costly than we initially anticipated. He's really apologetic about it and wants to make clear: Philly was a great trade partner. It was on our end."

Less notably, the Detroit Lions and New York Jets altered the compensation they originally agreed on in the Andre Cisco trade. The Lions wound up getting the safety for a 2029 sixth-rounder. Initially, Detroit was going to deal a 2028 sixth-round pick.

This has all led to a collection of front-office executives maintaining that trade terms shouldn't be changed once they're agreed upon, as reported by ESPN and NFL Network.

One possible solution, per that report, is the league preventing teams from altering draft picks following a trade agreement. In other words, teams would then only have the option to accept or reject a deal after a player's physical.

#2695. Tobias Harris Injury News: Return Timeline Revealed For Potential Season Debut

Fresh off their NBA Finals run this past summer, the San Antonio Spurs made a new addition to their backcourt: forward Tobias Harris. The elite wing scorer overstayed his welcome with the Detroit Pistons, leading him to sign with the Spurs for two years, $30.8 million. To this day, we've been waiting for his debut, and the moment may finally be in sight.

The 6'8″ NBA veteran initially went down during training camp after being diagnosed with a left calf strain, but the injury has gone beyond what anyone prepared for. Fortunately, as his recovery has progressed, a clearer picture of his timeline has finally emerged, with one insider claiming Harris could be back as soon as early December.

"Sources relayed to me that they [Spurs] did not expect this injury to linger on as long as it has right now," reported Chris Haynes. "But there is good news that I can report: I'm told he is getting better. Sources relayed to me that he is expected to start getting on the court for some practice work next week. If things progress, if everything goes as planned from next week when he steps on the floor, he could make his season debut as early as early December."

Hamstring injuries are notoriously tricky, and the recovery rates vary based on multiple factors. Still, Harris' absence is notably longer than the average, sparking some concern that his health might never be the same. If what Haynes says is true, then it means he's at least progressing in the right direction. Barring any setbacks, we could see Tobias Harris on the court as soon as next month, and it carries massive implications for the Western Conference hierarchy.

Harris, 34, is a longtime league veteran and naturally gifted scorer who can stretch the floor and create his own shot. After 15 seasons, he's no longer in his prime, but the Spurs believe he can still fill their hole on the wing, just like he did for the No. 1-seeded Pistons last season, when he averaged 13.3 points, 5.1 rebounds, 2.5 assists, 0.9 steals, and 0.4 blocks per game on 46.9% shooting from the field over 63 games.

For a Spurs team that's 11-2, there's no use rushing Harris back. They're the hottest team in the league right now (11 straight wins) and coming off a ridiculously lopsided victory (123-85) over the Golden State Warriors. Sans Harris, the Spurs have done just fine with Stephon Castle and Devin Vassell on the wing. Off the bench, 25-year-old Julian Champagnie has provided a consistent spark.

So, yes, the Spurs may not need Tobias Harris to prosper this season, but adding his scoring and veteran leadership to the locker room will only raise their ceiling further. At the very least, it will add depth to their already deep rotation.

What can we expect from Harris himself? Probably not much, especially out of the gate as he shakes off the rust from an extended hiatus. Fortunately, he'll have all the time in the world to get his rhythm back and make a real impact for this red-hot Spurs team.

#2693. Nets are learning the hard way that their offense can’t rely only on Julius Randle

MEMPHIS, Tenn. — It's still early, but the Nets have had a couple of weeks of data to learn this new team, to see what works and what gets them worked over.

What they've found out the hard way was less Julius Randle iso ball and more dribble handoffs, less watching and more moving.

"It's a little bit of both. Earlier in the year, obviously you're trying to [learn]," Michael Porter Jr. admitted heading into Saturday's tilt at Memphis. "We didn't get a chance to play a lot of games with Julius. But Julius doesn't want to sit around and iso all game, either. So we've had conversations in film sessions where we're just seeing what works, and then we're trying to use each player to their skill sets.

"I know Day'Ron [Sharpe] can really pass the ball as well. He can, he's good in the [dribble handoff]. So, him getting more usage out of that is helping him play better as well. So I think that we're a ball-movement and a player-movement team. And then we have the luxury of having Julius, who can get those tough buckets off the dribble in those iso situations. So it's a balance."

Brooklyn was all kinds of unbalanced to start the season.

Between poor spacing, a lack of movement — both the ball sticking and players not running to their spots — and a certain amount of natural deferring to the Nets' only All-Star and their best on-ball scorer since the Kevin Durant-Kyrie Irving era, the offense stagnated.

Brooklyn Nets forward Julius Randle (30) goes to the basket past New Orleans Pelicans center Derik Queen (22) in New Orleans, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. AP Photo/Tyler Kaufman

Through their first five games, the Nets went winless and looked terrible. Randle was averaging 5.4 isos — granted, often catching so-called grenades as the shot clock wound down. Their Offensive Rating sat at a wretched 104.8, and the fifth-worst Net Rating in the league.

But they've looked much better in splitting the past six games. Randle's isos have more than halved down to 2.3. Instead, Porter leads the league in DHOs at 5.1; but he's seen them bump from 4.6 to 5.5, per Clutchpoints. And Sharpe has seen his assists vault from 1.2 in the winless start to 6.5 in the 3-3 run since.

The Nets have been the beneficiary coming into the Grizzlies game, with the Offensive Rating spiking to 113.4 and their Net Rating vaulting to a more solid 15th.

"We've always been running our offense like that. The first couple games of the season, it was like we got a lot of new players. So we're trying to figure out each other's game, especially with Julius," Sharpe said. "He brings a lot of gravity, so I'm trying to find out how to play off him. We're all trying to figure out how to play off of him. After film, after talks, we figured out what's working best for us. And I feel we just keep doing that and keep building towards that."

Julius Randle of the Brooklyn Nets looks to pass during a game against the New Orleans Pelicans in New Orleans, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Nets divide their season into five-game blocks. And it took a winless first section for them to figure out what worked, and what was getting them worked over.

Brooklyn coach Jordi Fernández said it wasn't so much a wholesale change of the playbook or what was being run. As the Nets learned each other, they've just started running those same plays better, and with more conviction and precision.

"We run the same plays, but they were not run with the same purpose," Fernández said. "You see now Julius is finishing at the rim, how decisive he is with his drives, how much [more] active [Porter] has been with his cutting and mixing up his cuts and coming to the ball, and also Day'Ron catching it low and feeling comfortable with assisting or making those passes.

"So it just speaks of the work that the group is putting in, adjusting who I am in this new group and how can I be better for the group? And all these guys that I've mentioned — not just these three — but everybody is trying to do the right things not for themselves but for the group."

#2690. Ground stop nudges Ole Miss-Oklahoma game to Sunday (and OU doesn't like that)

Friday’s nationwide shutdown of the aviation system has resulted in a delayed kickoff for several college football games on Saturday. One of them was moved to Sunday — and the home team isn’t happy.

The Ole Miss visit to Oklahoma shifted from 4:15 p.m. ET on Saturday to Sunday at 1:00 p.m. ET.

“The Ole Miss football team was unable to depart Oxford Friday night due to the FAA ground stoppage,” the SEC said in a statement. “After the stoppage was lifted, a late-night departure could not be accommodated by the charter company with which Ole Miss was contracted and the team’s departure has been delayed until Saturday afternoon.”

Oklahoma athletic director Roger Denny strenuously objected to the decision to move the game to Sunday.

“After multiple conversations with the league office, Commissioner [Greg] Sankey informed us of his decision to move our game to Sunday, over our strong objection,” Denny said. “He further informed us that our failure to play on Sunday at the directed time would result in a forfeit. I understand that difficult decisions sometimes must be made under extraordinary circumstances. But those decisions must also consider the impact on everyone involved. Put simply, this decision isn’t fair to our athletes, our institution, or to Sooner Nation.”

Oklahoma nevertheless will comply with the decision. Even if the Sooners don’t like the decision to play the game later.

“Rescheduling the game has a significant impact, particularly on our fans,” Denny said. “Those who have made travel arrangements, accommodations, and personal plans around a Saturday game now face unnecessary disruption and hardship. Their time, commitment, and investment deserve greater consideration than they were afforded in the conference’s decision tonight. . . . We are obligated to play the game when directed. But we remain steadfast in our belief that the wrong outcome was reached this evening.”

On one hand, there’s no point huffing and puffing if Oklahoma ultimately wasn’t going to blow the house down. On the other hand, the school may have felt compelled to do something to deflect the frustrations of those who weren’t happy with having the game moved to the next day.

The bottom line is that the ground stop created travel disruptions and inconveniences for millions of people. And few are thrilled about that.

Oklahoma chose to complain. If it makes them feel better, fine. In the end, it doesn’t change the fact that the game will be added to the cluster of NFL games that begin at the same time on Sunday afternoon.

Los Angeles Lakers' Luka Doncic (77) reacts during the second half of an NBA basketball game against the Milwaukee Bucks in Milwaukee, Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

#2687. Lions vs. Patriots 5 Qs preview: Can Drake Maye clean up turnovers against Detroit in Germany?

For their first game in 11 days, the Detroit Lions take their show on the road across the Atlantic for an international Week 10 showdown in Munich, Germany, facing off against the defending AFC Champion New England Patriots.

We had the pleasure of connecting with Taylor Kyles, a staff writer for Pats Pulpit, to give us some insight into this New England squad ahead of Sunday morning's tilt. We learned about the growing concern surrounding Drake Maye's turnover regression, how injuries have decimated New England's offensive line, and the coverage busts plaguing an otherwise physical defense—plus how Detroit's offensive weapons could exploit the Patriots' secondary in Munich:

1. The Patriots have had a bit of a Jekyll and Hyde season through nine games. It looked like they were hitting their stride after beating the Bills, Raiders, and Jets, but since then, they've dropped two of their last three games.

As the defending AFC Champions, what's most concerning about this uneven start to 2026? What's there to be encouraged by in their first half of 2026?

The most discouraging thing has been their inconsistency against upper-echelon teams outside of their division. The defense looks great against mid-tier passing attacks, but some of their schematic quirks (not using as much press and man as expected for their personnel, heavy use of disguises, spinning the dial with coverages) have been exposed against the league's best passers. Drake Maye also regressed to his turnover-prone ways last week after over a month of clean football and MVP-caliber play.

On a positive note, Maye looked good outside of his interceptions last week while fighting for his life against the Packers' pressure packages, so cutting back on poor decisions and throws should allow him to return to his more recent form. Defensively, they have the players to perform better than what we've seen, but I think the coaching staff needs to do a better job putting players in better positions to succeed and potentially simplifying things so players can do what they do best.

2. Drake Maye was six voting points away from being the league's MVP a season ago, throwing 31 touchdowns to just 8 interceptions. 2026 has seen some pretty volatile play from Maye on a week-to-week basis, leading to him already surpassing his turnover total from a year ago through nine games.

Do you see Maye regressing from a decision-making standpoint, or is his 2026 season a victim of circumstances beyond his control?

A relatively lackluster supporting cast and a banged-up offensive line have made life difficult for Maye. A.J. Brown and Romeo Doubs haven't lived up to expectations when on the field, and blockers Mike Onwenu, Alijah Vera-Tucker, and Reggie Gilliam are all currently on injured reserve. That said, most of Maye's turnovers resulted from avoidable mistakes, such as forced attempts and throwing the wrong kind of pass for the situation. Maye winning Offensive Player of the Month in October showed he can rise above these issues, but we have yet to see him do it for a truly extended stretch.

3. Injuries have plagued the Patriots all season long. Where do you feel the effects of injuries are hurting this team the most? Who are some players that have made the most out of their newfound opportunities?

Injuries have most profoundly impacted the offensive line. The ground game has been limited due to the loss of both starting guards, forcing the Patriots into being a fairly straightforward downhill rushing attack. Josh McDaniel has tried incorporating different looks and wrinkles, but outside zone hasn't really been an option with so little chemistry up front. Pass protection held up surprisingly well for a bit, but schemed pressures like blitzes and stunts have become increasingly problematic over the past three weeks, resulting in far too many unblocked pressures. There is hope things will settle down if veteran Greg Van Roten returns from IR, but he's missed significant time and is one of the oldest players on the team, so how he performs down the stretch is anyone's guess. Matt Pryor has emerged as a relative bright spot beside Morgan Moses, who's been dealing with a foot injury for weeks, but the big-bodied guard can be exposed if forced to quickly redirect.

4. New England's defense is a super interesting read from a statistical perspective. They have one of the most effective pass rushes in the NFL, and their run defense is a top-10 unit at minimum, yet they're average as a whole unit by DVOA (15th) and below average in EPA/play (23rd).

When the Patriots defense is at its best, what are they doing successfully? Where are New England's weaknesses on this side of the ball? Are there certain positional matchups, or player archetypes they struggle in defending?

Physicality and discipline stand out most when the Patriots are playing at their best. It's a veteran group that plays with a lot of pride and attitude, and that translates into players swarming to the football and laying the wood when given the opportunity. Unfortunately, communication lapses and issues against tempo have led to coverage busts and big plays for opposing offenses. The defense has also struggled to take the ball away this season, which has made life tougher for both sides of the ball. This week, I'm worried about their ability to defend Jahmyr Gibbs out of the backfield. New England has yet to face an opponent that truly fed a talented receiving back through the air, but I'm not sure the defense has the linebacker or safety depth to keep Gibbs quiet if Detroit presses the issue. Jameson Williams could also have himself a day if the secondary doesn't actively disrupt his releases and route stems.

5. The Patriots opened as 2.5-point home underdogs at FanDuel Sportsbook heading into this international matchup against the Lions. What's one thing you want to see, what's one thing you need to see, and one thing you're sure you'll see happen in this matchup between the Lions and Patriots on Sunday morning our time?

I'd like to see Drake Maye play a clean game and go toe-to-toe with Jared Goff. He had the talent to do so and elevate his supporting cast, but it will ultimately come down to picking his spots and avoiding bad decisions. I need to see a more competitive effort from the defense, particularly in the red area. This feels like a bend-but-don't-break kind of game, so holding the Lions to field goals will be crucial. Finally, I'm sure I'll see the Lions move the ball effectively. There are simply too many weapons for the Patriots to defend consistently, particularly without Carlton Davis. Goff will take what the defense gives him and hit a handful of shots, but again, the Patriots must be competitive once the Lions reach the red area.

#2683. Mikal Bridges follows through in first game after promise to ‘be better’ for Knicks

INDIANAPOLIS — Mikal Bridges repeated it eight times in a three-minute interview Friday: "Be better."

The floundering shooting guard took ownership of his struggles before a rejuvenating performance in Friday's 106-104 loss to the Pacers, saying he's the lone problem and solution.

"I just look in the mirror, I just got to be better," said Bridges, who scored a season-high 18 points on 8-of-12 shooting Friday. "Nobody else to blame but me, and I just got to go out there and, whatever it is, just be better and go harder."

Bridges was light on specifics, but his shooting numbers were ugly. Before Friday, he was at 32.9 percent from the field overall and 26.5 percent on 3-pointers — despite many of his looks being wide open.

Mikal Bridges (25) of the New York Knicks dribbles against the Indiana Pacers during an NBA Cup game, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026, in Indianapolis. NBAE via Getty Images

Head coach Mike Brown responded by benching Bridges for long stretches, preferring to close games with reserve Landry Shamet.

"When times are tough, you just gotta own up to it and just be better," Bridges said.

After Bridges played just 17 minutes in Wednesday's loss to the Cavaliers — the fourth time in 11 games that he's logged 17 or fewer — Brown said it'd be "hard to find minutes" for Bridges and "even for times for Josh Hart."

Brown reasoned that reserves Shamet and Deuce McBride are playing well.

But the Knicks also have $150 million tied up in Bridges, who said he's not relying on flipping a switch to excellence in the playoffs like the past two seasons.

"I try to be my best throughout the whole year. Obviously, struggles come, which obviously nobody wants, but like I said, in these moments, gotta look in the mirror, and you're tested the most and just see what you're made of," Bridges said. "So, just gotta be better."

Bridges had a different tune last season during a slump, when he blamed a lack of opportunity for his performance decline.

"Sometimes you try to get open, and sometimes it doesn't find me. Just try to find ways to stay aggressive," Bridges said in March, a little more than a month before he started a dominant playoff run. "That's it."

This time, Bridges was self-scrutinizing.

New York Knicks forward Mikal Bridges (25) shoots against the Indiana Pacers during an NBA basketball game in Indianapolis, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"I got to do everything better," he said.

Brown said he hasn't thought about removing Bridges from the starting lineup because he's a proven vet and it'd be a "panic decision" so early in the season. But there's a minutes crunch at the wing with Bridges, Hart, Shamet and McBride.

On Friday, Bridges played well, and Hart (zero points, 0-for-4 shooting) was benched.

"There are several different ways you can look at it," Hart said. "You can look at it, if [Shamet and McBride] have it going, cool. Good. You could you look at it like, 'OK, maybe something else has to change [like a trade].'

"But there's going to good days with that, bad days."

Free-throw discrepancy was a storyline in the first half. The Knicks took just five free throws in the opening two quarters; the Pacers quadrupled that amount with 20. Over the entire game, the Pacers shot 25 free throws to the Knicks' 13.

Frustrations boiled over late in the second quarter when Brown berated a referee over a non-call. Brown was irate and picked up a technical foul. The Knicks trailed at the break, 58-49.

"I did get a tech. But the reality was the Pacers were playing harder than us. It was out of frustration," Brown said. "Some of it because … we have a guy like Jalen who is an All-Star and an MVP candidate, and he takes zero free throws in the first half. … That, in itself, is frustrating to know that we're getting beat that bad at the free-throw line and they're missing some calls. Are we fouling them? Yes. And we have to do a better job earlier. But in the same breath, the discrepancy shouldn't be that bad two games in a row."

Brown also lamented the free-throw discrepancy in Wednesday's loss to the Cavaliers.

#2682. Jerry Jones: "I don't think we're far away" from 18 regular-season games

As the NFL embarks on the second half of its current 18-week regular-season schedule, it’s just a matter of time before the current structure changes.

It’s no secret that the NFL wants to expand the regular season from 17 to 18 games. The league is sufficiently confident about it happening that folks like Cowboys owner Jerry Jones speak of it as a given.

“I don’t think we’re far away from it,” Jones said Friday on 105.3 The Fan in Dallas, regarding the addition of an extra game. “I don’t know how — what is far away. Next month? No. Next year? Maybe. [The] next year? Maybe. That has to be negotiated with the players.

“But it’s in everybody’s best interests.  You’d cut back on the preseason games. You’d have teams more develop in the first few games as the type of thing you were trying to get done in preseason. But it’s in everybody’s interest, and it certainly is in the players’ best interest in my mind, for sure.  And having said that, I would anticipate that coming.”

His comments presume the NFL Players Association will happily agree to 18 regular-season games. They likely won’t. At a minimum, they’ll seek major concessions in exchange for the willingness to add yet another game that counts.

The unspoken goal had been 2027. The NFL delayed picking a date for Super Bowl LXII in Atlanta in order to accommodate the possibility that the game will slide a week or two deeper into February. That would make 2028 the earliest year for expansion, as a practical matter. (Adding a game for 2027 would be possible only if the dead week between the conference championships is wiped out or if the season starts a week earlier. If an 18th game will result in a second bye, both would be required.)

Expansion by 2027 would require expedited negotiations. Expansion by 2028 would hinge on the NFL and NFLPA striking a deal within the next year or so. The union has shown no inclination, however, to engage in talks aimed at extending the current Collective Bargaining Agreement, which runs through the 2030 season.

Still, if push comes to shove, there will be 18 games. The blueprint was created in 2011. The NFL will lock the players out until the players agree to the things the owners want. Most notably, 18 regular-season games and 16 annual international games.

Beyond that, Jones’s comment about the early games of the season is odd. “You’d have teams more develop in the first few games as the type of thing you were trying to get done in preseason.”

He says that as if it’s a good thing. It definitely is not. Already, teams struggle in September to get up to speed, given that most teams (with the exception of those assigned to the Hall of Fame Game) play only three preseason games. When the preseason reduces to two games, that’s even fewer chances to prepare.

It’s also fewer chances for the non-starters to get the attention of coaches and General Managers in games that don’t count in the standings. It will become harder for undrafted free agents to make the 53-man rosters.

Regardless, the motivation isn’t football. It’s money. More inventory equals more weekends equals more broadcast windows equals more revenue.

And that likely will continue until the preseason shrinks to zero games — and the regular season expands to 20 games.
